Sprint 2 closed NEW-02: every `ToolRegistry::call()` now passes a default-deny gate that asks the same matrix with the **caller's own** role, on both transports (`chat-engine` sets the actor from the session exactly as `server.php` sets it from the credential). The amplification the exclusion existed to prevent is gone, so the exclusion has nothing left to protect. D-035 wrote its own revisit trigger — "Sprint 2 close" — and this is it.
- **Why `viewer` stays out:** a read-only role has no authoring work for an agent to do, and the gate would refuse nearly everything it asked for — a surface that mostly answers "no" is a worse experience than one the role does not see. Not a security judgement; a product one. It is a one-line change if a requirement appears.
- **Proven, not asserted, and proven in the direction that matters:** both new expectations were run against the un-widened matrix first and **failed** — `AdminGateHttpTest` reported *"installer/admin/ai-chat.php as editor: expected 200, got 403"* on the real wire, and `AdminGateMapTest` failed on the matrix. The widening is asserted end to end (an editor's HTTP request to `ai-chat.php` returns 200; a viewer's returns 403), not by re-reading the map — the rule slice 4 of Sprint 1 set for the gate map.
- **The honest limit, stated rather than implied:** `Auth::login()` still validates only against `config['admin_user']` (**NEW-11**), so no editor account can actually log in to use this today. The widening is correct and takes effect the moment NEW-11 is fixed; it changes no reachable behaviour on a current install. Recorded here so a later reader does not mistake "no observable change in production" for "this decision did nothing".
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **keep owner+admin** (safe, but D-035's stated reason has expired, and an exclusion kept past its justification accumulates as unexplained restriction — the next reader inherits a rule with no live reason); **also widen to `viewer`** (nothing in the matrix makes a viewer's agent session useful, and it enlarges the surface for no requirement); **gate the chat on `mcp.manage` instead** (already rejected in D-035 for the right reason: it means "may mint MCP credentials", not "may consume AI", and the wrong mental model at exactly the point where the model matters).
- Supersedes: **D-035**. Depends on **D-046**/**D-048** (the gate and the map) being in force — if the MCP gate were ever removed, this widening would have to be reverted with it, and the comment at the `ai.use` row in `user-manager.php` says so.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-24, before commit — L-015): the `security-auditor` returned THREE blocking findings against this decision, two of them real, and the widening does not ship without the fix.** Both review subagents ran on the finished diff, docs included. The `code-reviewer` found no blocking issue (one stale why-comment in `admin-gate.php`, fixed). The `security-auditor` found what this decision's own justification had not checked: **`ai.use` does not bound what `ai-chat.php` exposes.** That page `require_once`s `partials/ai-panel-{$panel}.php` for a caller-supplied `?panel=`, and those four partials carried **zero** permission checks between them — `ai-panel-users.php` creates users and changes any account's password behind CSRF alone (work `users.php` reserves to `users.manage`, owner-only), `ai-panel-settings.php` writes site/email/SMTP settings (`site.configure`), and `api/ai-chat.php`'s `get_providers` returned `masked_key` (6+4 characters of every configured AI provider key) while its four sibling actions all require `site.configure`. Recorded as **NEW-31** and **fixed in this slice**: a panel→capability map in `ai-chat.php` mirroring each panel's standalone twin, absent = deny, running before `header.php`; and `get_providers` gated like its siblings. Proven both directions — against the unfixed code an editor got **200** on `?panel=users` and on `get_providers`.
  - **Two of the auditor's specific escalation routes do not exist and are recorded as refuted, not adopted:** `UserManager::create()` refuses a second owner and `update()` refuses `role = owner` outright, so "mint yourself an owner in one request" is blocked at the manager. The real path is create-an-`admin` plus `changePassword` on the owner's id — worse in effect, different in mechanism. Re-derived against source before acting (L-013).
  - **The honest sequencing point:** NEW-31 is **not** caused by this decision. It has been true since `ai.use` was created (D-035), and it is live for **`admin`** today, one tier above the role this decision adds. What D-051 did was make it *visible*, because `ai.use` and the panels' capabilities had been the identical owner+admin set until now — a check missing exactly where two capabilities coincide, for the third time in this project. Had the widening shipped without the audit, this decision's central claim ("an editor in the chat can do exactly what an editor may do, and no more") would have been **false**, which is the L-002 defect inside a security decision.
  - **The third finding is verified and deliberately retained:** `chat-engine`'s generic `catch (\Throwable)` surfaces `PermissionDeniedException::getMessage()` — the internal `denialReason()` text naming the caller's role and the required capability — to the chat UI and the model, where `server.php` deliberately sends the neutral catalogue string instead. Kept, with the reasoning recorded rather than re-litigated: the audience differs. The HTTP transport answers an arbitrary MCP client; the chat answers the operator's **own authenticated admin session**, which already knows its role, and the capability identifiers are **published documentation** in this open-source product (`docs/reference/authorization.md` prints the entire matrix). Nothing non-public crosses the boundary, and the model needs the reason to adapt. This matches the position already recorded in the slice-2 review follow-ups; it is not re-opened here.

## D-052 — D-029's version floors are stale and are raised to reach its own "audit to zero"; the re-vendor is three packages plus a seventeenth
- Date / phase: 2026-07-25 / Phase 5, Sprint 3 planning (user decision this session, on a question put before anything was changed)
- Decision: The NEW-05 remediation targets **`guzzlehttp/guzzle` 7.15.1**, **`guzzlehttp/psr7` 2.13.0** and **`guzzlehttp/promises` 2.5.1**, and accepts **`symfony/polyfill-php80`** as a new vendored package (16 → **17**). D-029's remediation *shape* is unchanged — hand-pin, re-vendor, re-audit to zero, update `LICENSE-THIRD-PARTY.md`, let `tests/Unit/VendorAiManifestTest.php` prove the four records agree. What changes is the two version numbers written inside it.
- **Why this is not re-opening D-029, which would be out of order:** D-029 records two things that no longer agree. It fixes the target as *"bump guzzle ≥ 7.12.1 and psr7 ≥ 2.12.1"* **and** as *"re-run `composer audit -d installer` to zero"*. Measured 2026-07-25, those floors leave **6 of 11 advisories open**: three guzzle advisories are fixed only in 7.15.1, one in 7.14.2, one in 7.12.3, and psr7's newest in 2.12.3. **L-014's rule decides it** — when a recorded remediation and a recorded acceptance criterion disagree, the criterion is the requirement and the remediation is a guess. The version numbers were one session's derivation from a 5-advisory list; "audit to zero" is the property the product must have. The criterion is honoured and the derivation is corrected.
- **Why the bump is wider than two packages, verified rather than assumed:** `guzzle 7.15.1` requires `guzzlehttp/psr7 ^2.13`, `guzzlehttp/promises ^2.5.1` and `symfony/polyfill-php80 ^1.25`. `installer/vendor-ai/symfony/` currently holds only `deprecation-contracts` and `polyfill-mbstring`, so the polyfill is genuinely new. psr7 2.12.3 would clear psr7's own advisories, but guzzle 7.15.1's `^2.13` makes 2.13.0 the resolvable answer anyway.
- **D-029's central cost claim survives, and that is what keeps this a re-vendor rather than a scope change:** `soukicz/llm` 0.5.0 — the only genuine root requirement — asks for `guzzlehttp/guzzle: ^7.9`, `guzzlehttp/psr7: ^2.7`, `guzzlehttp/promises: ^2.0` (`installer/vendor-ai/soukicz/llm/composer.json:23-31`, read directly, not taken from the lock). All three targets satisfy it. No dependency-tree surgery.
- **The PHP floor does not move, so this decision does not touch the support matrix.** The 8.3 requirement comes from `soukicz/llm` (`php: >=8.3`), which is untouched; `config.platform.php = 8.3.0` and the generated `vendor-ai/composer/platform_check.php` regenerate identically. NEW-06 is neither created nor closed by the bump — it is closed on its own merits (D-053).
- **D-029's stated command is wrong and is corrected here rather than followed:** it says `composer install -d installer`, which installs *what the lock already says* and would reinstall 7.10.0. The pins are hand-edited in `installer/composer.json` first, then `composer update`. They must remain **exact versions with no operator**, because `VendorAiManifestTest::normalise()` strips only a leading `v` — a constraint written `^7.15` fails `assertSame` against the vendored `7.15.1`.
- **The reachability assessment is redone from scratch, not inherited, and it changes one recorded conclusion.** D-029's assessment turned on *"no cookie jar, no user-controllable URL in the AI module"*, which does not cover the new URI/host-parsing advisories. Re-derived, with every decisive claim re-verified by hand after a subagent reported it (L-013):
  - **The feared NEW-15 adjacency does not exist.** `installer/core/safe-http.php` and `installer/core/http-client.php` contain **zero** references to `GuzzleHttp` or `Psr\Http` — they are pure first-party parsing (`parse_url`, `filter_var`, `inet_pton`, `gethostbynamel`, `dns_get_record`) over cURL and stream transports. "Host confusion via authority reinterpretation" needs one URL parsed by two different parsers, and the two stacks never share a URL. **CVE-2026-59882 and CVE-2026-48998 have no bearing on the SSRF control**, which is the specific question this slice was told to answer rather than inherit.
  - **Cookies (4 advisories): no path.** The string `cookie` occurs nowhere in `installer/core/ai/` or `installer/vendor-ai/soukicz/llm/`; Guzzle's default is `'cookies' => false` (`vendor-ai/guzzlehttp/guzzle/src/Client.php:236`) and the jar is built only on `=== true` (`:260-261`).
  - **Proxy (2 advisories): no attacker-reachable path.** Nothing sets `proxy`; the env read is SAPI-guarded for `HTTP_PROXY`, and no request header can produce a `$_SERVER` key named exactly `HTTPS_PROXY` under CGI. Operator misconfiguration only — which is what D-029 already said about CVE-2026-55568 and what this bump fixes.
  - **Referer/fragment: no path** (`allow_redirects` defaults `'referer' => false`, the header is stripped, no caller overrides it). **URI/host parsing: no exploitable path** — all five provider authorities are hardcoded literals and `chat-engine.php`'s `default => throw` pins the provider enum.
  - The only first-party Guzzle references repo-wide are two **catch clauses** (`installer/core/ai/chat-engine.php:197`, `:216`) — exception types, not URLs.
- **Stated plainly rather than softened: none of the 11 advisories has a demonstrated exploitation path in Klytos.** The bump happens because a self-updating CMS with a real installed base should not ship known-vulnerable dependencies and D-029 set a closing date — not because an exploit was found. Saying otherwise to justify the work would be the L-002 defect.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **the literal D-029 floors** (7.12.1 / 2.12.1 — smallest diff, no third package, no seventeenth package; rejected because `composer audit` would still report 6 advisories afterwards, so NEW-05 could not close and a second remediation would immediately queue behind the first); **guzzle 7.12.3 + psr7 2.12.5** (clears three more and still leaves four — there is no middle target that reaches zero, because the highest floor is 7.15.1); **guzzle 8.0.0** (exists, and `soukicz/llm`'s `^7.9` forbids it — that *would* be the dependency-tree rewrite D-029 ruled out); **accept permanently** (rejected on the same grounds D-029 rejected it — nothing in this project's triage has been written off as permanent).
- Supersedes: the version numbers and the `composer install` command in **D-029** only. D-029's shape, its rationale, its Estimate requirement and its rejection list all stand.
- **Implemented in slice 1 (2026-07-25), with three mechanism notes and one number corrected — outcome unchanged.**
  - **`composer audit -d installer` → `No security vulnerability advisories found.`** Resolution was exactly as planned: *1 install, 3 updates, 0 removals* — guzzle 7.10.0→7.15.1, promises 2.3.0→2.5.1, psr7 2.9.0→2.13.0, plus `symfony/polyfill-php80 v1.37.0`. **No unintended package moved**, verified by listing the changed directories rather than assuming the exact pins held. The two files that carry the fixes — `guzzle/src/Handler/ProxyEnvironment.php` and `psr7/src/Rfc3986.php` — are physically present in the tree.
  - **The diff is 95 files, not 482, and that correction matters because 482 was the argument.** D-029 called this "a change across 482 tracked files" and used that figure to justify treating remediation as a scope change rather than a slice. 482 is the size of the whole vendored tree; the change is **95 files across `installer/`** — of which **93 are under `vendor-ai/` (27 added, 66 modified, 0 deleted)** and the other two are `installer/composer.json` and `installer/composer.lock`. *(The two scopes are stated explicitly because 27+66+0 = 93, not 95, and a reader re-deriving the figure per L-015 would otherwise hit an apparent contradiction — caught by the slice's own `code-reviewer` pass.)* The tree grows 482 → **509** tracked files. Nobody had measured it — the number was carried from the tree size to the change size and reused as a cost argument (L-015: a number copied from another document is not a measurement). The decision to give this its own slice was still right, for the *verification* reason D-029 also gave; the file count simply was not the evidence for it.
  - **`installer/composer.json` gains `"version": "1.0.0"`, and it is load-bearing rather than tidy.** Left alone, Composer resolved the root package's identity from git and wrote `'pretty_version' => 'dev-develop'` plus `'reference' => '<current commit sha>'` into the **tracked**, generated `vendor-ai/composer/installed.php`. That makes a tracked artifact depend on the branch name and the commit it happened to be regenerated at — directly against this manifest's own recorded purpose ("reproduced byte-for-byte", D-028). Pinning the root version restores `'pretty_version' => '1.0.0'` and `'reference' => null`, so the file is a function of the dependency set and nothing else.
  - **The drift guard had a hardcoded assumption that had never been exercised, and this is the transferable part.** `VendorAiManifestTest::vendoredVersions()` skipped the root package by the literal string `'__root__'`. That is only what Composer writes for a root package it *cannot name*; this manifest has a name, so the first `composer update` this repository has ever actually run renamed the entry to `klytos/vendor-ai-manifest` and the root leaked into all three comparisons. The guard was built by D-028 against a tree that had been vendored **elsewhere** and was explicitly never regenerated here (slice 2's own test point asserted "`vendor-ai/` unmutated"), so the assumption rode along untested for three sprints. Fixed by reading `$installed['root']['name']` — the documented intent, implemented rather than approximated. **Not a loosening:** both set comparisons stay `assertSame` in both directions, and a vendored package cannot collide with the root name because Composer refuses to install a package named after its own root. Recorded as **L-020**.
  - **The prove-it-fails cycle came free from the real work (L-016).** `VendorAiManifestTest` was observed failing on **all three** methods against the half-updated records, then green once each of the four was reconciled — no injected fault was needed. The new `tests/Integration/VendorAiCompatibilityTest.php` was proven to fail in two directions by probe (an unresolvable symbol; a URL psr7 normalises), and its cookie assertion was proven to read a property that genuinely can be non-`false` (`new Client(['cookies' => true])` yields a `CookieJar`), so it is not an assertion that can only ever pass.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-25, before commit — L-015): both subagents ran on the finished diff, docs included, and neither returned a blocking finding.** Recorded rather than folded in silently, because two of the four outcomes are corrections to my own text and one is a correction to a claim in this decision.
  - **`security-auditor`: no blocking findings, and the reachability verdict was re-derived rather than accepted.** It reproduced the "no path" conclusion independently for all three families it could test by inspection — cookies (`grep -i cookie` across `installer/core/ai/` and `soukicz/llm/src/` → zero; `Client::configureDefaults()` sets `'cookies' => false` and builds a jar only on `=== true`), proxy (`HttpClientFactory::createClient()` sets only `headers` and `handler`), referer (`RedirectMiddleware::$defaultSettings` has `'referer' => false` and the header is actively removed when not enabled) — and confirmed **zero** `GuzzleHttp`/`Psr\Http` references in `safe-http.php` and `http-client.php`, so the differential-parsing precondition for the host-confusion CVEs genuinely does not exist. It also confirmed the supply-chain checks: none of the four touched packages declares Composer `scripts`, and `exec`/`shell_exec`/`eval`/`base64_decode` appear nowhere in them.
  - **It found the `model` parameter defect is real and TWO of my framings of it were wrong.** Re-verified myself by running the encodings rather than taking either the reviewer's or my own word (L-013): a `#` in `$input['model']` pushes `:generateContent?key={apiKey}` into the URI **fragment**, which PSR-7 never puts on the wire — so the request reaches Google with **no API key at all**; a `?` pulls the same substring into the query and truncates the path. The **host never moves** in any spelling and CRLF is percent-encoded, so there is no header injection and no authority takeover. And the population is **not** "authenticated admin": `api/ai-chat.php` is gated at `ai.use`, which **D-051 widened to `editor`** — so editor and above. Recorded as its own audit finding with a trigger; not fixed here (adjacent subsystem, D-031's narrowing).
  - **`code-reviewer`: no blocking findings, and it caught a real precision defect in this decision — "95 files" and "27 added, 66 modified, 0 deleted" sum differently (93) because they are two different scopes.** Both figures were correct and their juxtaposition was not; a reader re-deriving them per L-015 would hit an apparent contradiction and have to consult a third document to resolve it. Corrected here and in the audit entry by naming the scope of each. It also caught that a comment in the new test said "three of the fourteen" are interfaces when four are — an uncounted number, in the same class, corrected.
  - **Confirmed sound against source rather than merely read:** the `VendorAiManifestTest` change is a correctness fix and not a loosening (a vendored package cannot collide with the root name, and a wrong root name still trips the set comparison rather than passing silently); `"version": "1.0.0"` has no effect on `composer audit`, resolution or the lock's `content-hash`; `REQUIRED_SYMBOLS` is **complete** — the reviewer re-ran the regeneration command in the test's own docblock and matched all 14; and `VendorAiCompatibilityTest::setUp()` registers no hook and writes no config, so it leaks into neither the D-042 hook baseline nor the D-039 config guard.
  - **One count reconciled rather than left as a discrepancy:** the filesystem holds **510** files under `vendor-ai/` against **509** tracked. The extra is `installer/vendor-ai/swaggest/json-schema/composer.lock`, excluded by `.gitignore:120` (`installer/vendor-ai/**/composer.lock`) — a pre-existing, deliberate rule, not drift.
  - **Both reviewers ran without a shell this session** and said so unprompted. That limit is recorded rather than glossed: neither could execute `git diff` or byte-compare against `HEAD`, so the file-count bookkeeping and the before/after `Client.php` comparison rest on measurements taken in the main session, not on an independent second run. Where the reviewers *could* verify by reading — the four-record agreement, the symbol list, the reachability greps — they did, and their conclusions are recorded above as their own.

## D-053 — The AI stack refuses an unsupported PHP before loading it, and advisories get standing detection in CI
- Date / phase: 2026-07-25 / Phase 5, Sprint 3 slice 2 (both halves were user decisions, taken at the sprint kickoff before anything was written)
- Decision: **(a)** `App::getChatEngine()` checks `PHP_VERSION_ID` **above** the `require_once` of `installer/vendor-ai/autoload.php` and throws a typed `Klytos\Core\Ai\UnsupportedRuntimeException` with a translated message when the runtime is below the vendored AI stack's floor. **(b)** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` gains a `vendor-advisories` job running `composer audit -d installer` with `continue-on-error: true`, so the current advisory list is printed on every CI run without failing the build.
- **Why (a) is a real defect and not tidying — audit NEW-06.** The old guard was `file_exists()`: a **presence** check standing in for a **version** check. Below PHP 8.3 the file exists, the require proceeds, and Composer's generated `platform_check.php` — required unconditionally by `autoload_real.php:25` — sends `HTTP/1.1 500`, echoes *"Composer detected issues in your platform"* **into the response body**, and throws a bare `\RuntimeException`. All of that happens inside vendored code, so the operator gets a 500 carrying third-party text with no indication that AI chat is the feature at fault or that the rest of the CMS is fine. Keel's "external dependencies fail safe" rule requires the opposite, and this was the only place in the product violating it.
- **Placement is the load-bearing part, and it is bounded on one side absolutely.** The guard cannot go below the `require_once`: by then `platform_check.php` has already fired, so a guard there is dead code that still reviews clean. Because no runtime test on a supported host can reach that branch, the ordering is pinned by a test that reads the source (`AiRuntimeGuardTest::testTheGuardRunsBeforeTheVendoredAutoloaderIsRequired`) — the same technique D-046 used to establish that the MCP gate sits above the `mcp.handle_tool` filter. **Proven by relocating the guard below the require and watching that test fail with its intended message, then reverting.**
- **The policy is a pure static, and that is not a style preference.** `App::aiRuntimeUnsupportedReason( int $phpVersionId ): ?string` takes the version as a parameter because **PHP cannot be downgraded inside the test suite**. A guard reading `PHP_VERSION_ID` directly would own a branch no test could ever reach — indistinguishable from a branch that *cannot* fire, which is L-010's failure mode. This is exactly the split D-044 made for `Auth::buildSecurityHeaders()`, for exactly the same reason. It returns a stable machine reason rather than a sentence (the `SafeHttp` `REASON_*` convention, D-041), so the caller owns the wording and the policy needs no I18n service to be testable.
- **The floor is written once and mechanically tied to its source.** `App::AI_MIN_PHP_VERSION_ID = 80300` has two upstream origins — `soukicz/llm`'s `"php": ">=8.3"` and the generated `vendor-ai/composer/platform_check.php` — and a test asserts the constant equals the value Composer actually generated. A future re-vendor that moves the floor therefore fails the suite rather than reaching a user as a 500. **The D-052 re-vendor did not move it**: `soukicz/llm` was untouched, so `platform_check.php` regenerated identically.
- **An action, never a filter** (`ai.runtime_unsupported`, carrying reason + required + running). A listener that could reverse this decision would be a listener that re-enables the fatal — the same reasoning behind `auth.access_denied` (D-032) and `http.safe.blocked` (D-041). **Nothing in core subscribes to it, and `docs/reference/ai-runtime.md` says so in those words**: L-019 was recorded because "it goes to the audit log" was true of a hook and false of the system, so this document states the seam/sink distinction at the moment of writing rather than waiting for a fresh-context pass to find it.
- **Why (b) is in this sprint at all.** The advisory count on the *same pinned versions* grew **5 → 11** between 2026-07-19 and 2026-07-24 and nothing detected it — it was noticed by hand at a sprint close, five days late, and it is the reason D-029's recorded floors had gone stale (D-052). Remediating without closing the detection gap would guarantee the same surprise next time.
- **Why the CI job does not fail the build, and why it is not a keel-verify check.** An advisory lands when *upstream publishes it*, not when someone pushes a commit, so a hard failure turns an unrelated pull request red for a reason its author cannot fix — and a check that cries wolf is one people learn to ignore, which is precisely the argument D-045 recorded for keel-verify's WARN tier. It stays out of `scripts/keel-verify` for a second reason: that script must keep working offline, and a network-dependent check would either fail or **go quiet** without a network — the failure mode L-010 and L-016 both record. Remediation stays a triaged user decision (D-022's standing rule), which is what produced D-029 and D-052 in the first place.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **wrap the `require_once` in try/catch instead of guarding above it** (smaller diff and superficially equivalent — but `platform_check.php` has already sent a 500 and echoed into the body by the time it throws, so the response is corrupted before anything can be caught; and catching a bare `\RuntimeException` there would swallow unrelated autoload failures); **raise the product's PHP floor to 8.3** (makes the mismatch disappear rather than handling it — a support-matrix change for a released product with an installed base, which belongs to D-027's trigger, not to a CVE sprint); **let `aiRuntimeUnsupportedReason()` read `PHP_VERSION_ID` itself** (one less parameter, and an untestable branch); **a filter to override the floor** (rejected outright — overriding it produces the fatal it exists to prevent); **a hard-failing CI check** (strongest signal, and the one most likely to be disabled the first time it blocks unrelated work); **a keel-verify check** (more consistent with checks 8 and 9, and it would make keel-verify network-dependent for the first time).
- **Docs granularity, recorded so it does not read as an omission:** `docs/api/INDEX.md` gained rows for the exception class and the action, but **not** for `App::AI_MIN_PHP_VERSION_ID` or `App::aiRuntimeUnsupportedReason()`. The INDEX lists classes, not their methods — no method row exists for any class in the file, `App::getChatEngine()` included — so adding two would break the established granularity rather than follow it. Both surfaces are documented in `docs/reference/ai-runtime.md` with runnable examples.
- **The refusal branch was driven end to end, and the first draft of this decision understated what could be proven.** It said the branch was unreachable on a supported host. The runtime cannot be lowered — but the **floor can be raised**, and temporarily setting `AI_MIN_PHP_VERSION_ID = 99999` drives the real path. That probe established four things nothing else did: the exception is the typed one; **`__()` resolves in the throwing path**, returning the catalogue value with both placeholders substituted rather than a raw key or an "undefined function" fatal (the sharpest risk here — NEW-18 means the *global* `__()` exists only under `admin/`, and one of the three callers is an MCP tool that never loads `admin/bootstrap.php`); the action fires **before** the throw and cannot influence it; and `GuzzleHttp\Client` is **not loaded**, which is a *runtime* proof of the ordering independent of the source-order assertion. The message half is now pinned permanently by `testTheRefusalMessageResolvesAndSubstitutesBothPlaceholders()`, proven to fail by removing the `ai` domain from `en.json` — `I18n::get()` returns the key verbatim on a miss, so "not the key" is exactly the right property to assert. **What is still unproven and is not claimed:** nobody has run this on a real PHP 8.1/8.2 host. Every property has been observed with the floor moved, not the runtime moved; the two differ only in the one value the policy reads, so the gap is small but real.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-25, before commit — L-015): the `security-auditor` returned no blocking findings, corrected one assumption in this decision, and found a second instance of a defect class this sprint had only recorded once.**
  - **The disclosure assumption was wrong in its premise and right in its conclusion.** This decision implied the refusal message is mostly logged; in fact **all three callers echo `$e->getMessage()` back to the caller** (`admin/api/ai-chat.php:333`, `admin/api/translations-ai.php:140`, `core/mcp/tools/translation-tools.php:282`). It is still fine, for a reason worth recording rather than assuming: `PHP_VERSION` is **already printed on the admin dashboard** (`admin/index.php:103`), which is gated at `pages.view` — held by **all four roles, viewer included** — while the three new callers are gated at `ai.use` (owner/admin/editor, D-051) or `site.configure` (owner/admin). The new message's audience is a strict **subset** of the audience that already sees the same value, and the required version is a public constraint in a committed `composer.json`. Conclusion unchanged, premise corrected.
  - **The old behaviour was the WORSE disclosure, which strengthens rather than merely permits this change.** `platform_check.php` set `HTTP/1.1 500` and **echoed** the message plus `PHP_VERSION` into the response body whenever `display_errors` is off — unconditionally, outside any `try/catch` a caller could interpose, corrupting whatever JSON body the caller was building. The new path emits one clean `jsonResponse(..., 500)`.
  - **Fail-open re-derived independently and found closed:** one call site for the vendored autoload repo-wide; the guard unconditional above it; `klytos_do_action()` returns `void` so no listener can alter control flow; `$this->chatEngine` assigned in exactly one place.
  - **The CI job is clean and inherits the existing hardening** — `pull_request` not `pull_request_target`, top-level `permissions: contents: read` with no job-level elevation, no secrets, no `github.*` interpolation into any `run:` block, and `continue-on-error` scoped to this job alone. **One property named rather than left implicit:** because the job always reports green, wiring it into required-status-check branch protection under the reading "green = no advisories" would be false. It is named `(report only)` and the trade-off is documented in-file, so this is an accepted design choice — recorded here so a future reader does not mistake it for a gate.
  - **It found a SECOND instance of NEW-34's defect class, plus the systemic reason for it — recorded as NEW-35 after independent verification.** `ToolRegistry::call()` performs the authorization gate, the plugin filter and the handler invocation, and **never validates `$params` against the tool's `inputSchema`** — read the method end to end to confirm it, rather than taking the report. So every `enum`/`type`/`required` a tool publishes is advisory, and `installer/core/ai-image-generator.php:62` interpolates an unvalidated `model` into a provider URL exactly as the chat path does, reachable over MCP via `klytos_generate_image`. The systemic half is the important one: fixing the interpolation alone would leave every other tool's declared contract unenforced. Not fixed here — it is outside this sprint's diff and enforcing schemas is a **behaviour change for every MCP client**, which needs the D-034 treatment.
  - **It also closed three angles on NEW-34 that I had not checked**, each verified against source: the OpenAI-compatible providers carry the model in the JSON body, not the URL, so the vector does not exist there; an unknown `provider` cannot be paired with a live key; and `klytos_translate_with_ai` never forwards a caller-supplied `model`.
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **NEW-06**; the standing-detection half closes the gap that let **NEW-05**'s advisory count grow unobserved.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-25, before commit — L-015): the `code-reviewer` returned a BLOCKING finding that was correct, and it was this slice's own subject turned against it.** Recorded in full because the shape is the transferable part.
  - **BLOCKING, and right: the two new integration tests would have turned CI's PHP 8.2 leg red.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs the FULL suite on 8.2 as well as 8.3, and `installer/vendor-ai/` needs 8.3 — so any test reaching `getChatEngine()` fails there. `testThisRuntimeIsSupportedSoTheGuardMustBeTransparent()` had been written as an *assertion* that the runtime was supported, which is a **failure** on 8.2 for behaviour that is **correct** on 8.2. A slice whose entire subject is "below 8.3 must degrade gracefully" shipped tests that break the build below 8.3.
  - **Measured rather than argued, by simulating the leg instead of guessing at it:** with `AI_MIN_PHP_VERSION_ID` temporarily raised so this host counts as unsupported, **8 tests across 3 classes** broke — 2 from this slice, **3 from slice 1**, and **3 that have been latent since Sprint 2 slice 3** (`ChatEngineToolListTest`). The reviewer flagged the pre-existing pair as context; the simulation is what turned that into a count.
  - **Why it had never been observed — recorded as L-022: CI has never run.** The workflow was written in Sprint 1 slice 9 and every commit since (29 at the time of writing) is unpushed by standing instruction. A committed workflow that is never triggered checks nothing; "CI runs the same commands the test points run" was true of the file and false of the project. This is **L-019 one level out** — a seam with no subscriber and a workflow with no run fail identically.
  - **The fix is two layers, because they answer different questions, and the first choice was rejected on the record.** Simply letting the 8 tests skip does NOT work: CI promotes any skip to a hard failure (D-045), a rule that exists to catch an un-seeded playground and must keep meaning exactly that. So the 8 carry `#[Group('ai-runtime')]` and CI's 8.2 leg **excludes** them explicitly, leaving D-045's rule intact and honest; and `IntegrationTestCase::requireAiRuntime()` — built in the shape of the existing `requirePlayground()` rather than as a second mechanism — skips them for a developer running the suite locally on 8.1/8.2. The group is applied **per method** in `AiRuntimeGuardIntegrationTest`, so the one test asserting only that the refusal *message* resolves keeps running on 8.2, which is the runtime where that message is what an operator actually sees. Proven: 8 errors/failures → **8 skips**; `--group ai-runtime` selects exactly 8; `--exclude-group` leaves 198 green.
  - **NON-BLOCKING, taken: the advisory job conflated "the audit failed" with "advisories found".** Piping `composer audit` into `grep` reports GREP's status, so no network or a missing lock would have been announced as a security finding. It now captures output and exit status separately and distinguishes three outcomes — clean / advisories / **"the audit did not complete, nothing was verified"** — each proven by feeding the branch its real string. Misreporting an infrastructure failure as a finding is the same dishonesty as the reverse, in a job whose own comments cite L-016 about it.
  - **NON-BLOCKING, taken: a stale line reference this diff itself created.** Inserting ~107 lines into `app.php` moved `getChatEngine()`, leaving `app.php:1009` wrong in `docs/04-adoption-audit.md` and `docs/03-technical-plan.md`. Both now name the method instead of a line number, so the next insertion cannot rot them again.
  - **NON-BLOCKING, REFUTED — and the refutation matters more than the item.** The reviewer asked for the new exception's `parent::__construct( $message )` to drop its inner-paren spaces to match `installer/core/ai/`'s neighbours. That contradicts the **recorded** convention: `docs/03-technical-plan.md` §3 says spaces inside parentheses are the project's style and *"do not 'correct' it"*, and `.claude/rules/code-style.md` names that section as the source of truth on conflict. I applied the change before checking, then reverted it. **L-013 and L-015 in one move** — a reviewer's suggestion is a hypothesis, and the project's own recorded convention outranks a neighbouring file's habit.
  - **Confirmed sound against source by the reviewer, not merely read:** the guard's placement and completeness (one call site for the vendored autoload, no bypass); `PHP_VERSION_ID` as the right comparison; all three callers catching `\Throwable` without leaking a stack trace; **the `__()` reachability reasoning, re-derived independently** and reaching the same conclusion (resolution follows the namespace of the file where the call is written, not the caller's); all 20 catalogues structurally valid, placeholders spelled identically, and **no missing accents, ñ or ¿/¡ in any of the 20** — checked individually; and every doc figure re-derived, including the INDEX counts (101 / 308 / 957).

## D-054 — Actions are fire-and-forget, enforced at registration; page data is modified through a filter
- Date / phase: 2026-07-25 / Phase 5, Sprint 4 slice 1 (the mutation mechanism, the enforcement severity and the sprint scope were all user decisions taken at the kickoff, before anything was written)
- Decision: **(a)** `Hooks::addAction()` and `Hooks::addFilter()` reflect the callback and throw a typed `Klytos\Core\HookContractException` when it declares a **by-reference parameter**, naming the hook, the parameter position and name, and the callback's `file:line`. **(b)** `PageManager` applies a new **`page.save_data`** filter to the record immediately before it is written, on create and on update, and **above** the pre-existing `page.before_save` action — which stays, unchanged, as an observer. **(c)** `x402-bootstrap.php`'s by-reference listener becomes a filter on `page.save_data`.
- **Why the obvious fix is refuted rather than merely disfavoured, and this is the load-bearing part.** D-026 deferred NEW-03 because the fix "changes the signature of `Hooks::doAction()`". Measured this session by running PHP rather than reasoning about it: `mixed &...$args` **does** bind by reference — and then PHP refuses any **non-variable** argument with a fatal `Error`. String and integer literals, `null`, array literals, class constants, concatenations, ternaries and `??` expressions all fatal; a function-call result emits a notice. **36+ call sites pass such an argument**, starting with `PageManager::create()`'s own `'create'` literal and including nine `$page ?? null` sites in `page-editor.php`. Worse, an **undefined** array key passed to a by-reference parameter is silently *created* in the caller's array, so `doAction( 'x', $data['maybe'] )` would materialise `$data['maybe'] = null`. There is no version of the by-reference variadic that is safe here.
- **Why enforcement is a throw and not a log.** A listener silently not registered is the by-omission failure the throw exists to end — the plugin author sees nothing and the site behaves as if their code were absent. The throw is contained where it matters: `PluginLoader::loadPlugin()` wraps every plugin entry point in `try/catch (\Throwable)` and records a named load error (`plugin-loader.php:245-252`), so a third-party plugin carrying such a listener **fails to load with a readable reason rather than taking the CMS down**. Verified against source, not assumed.
- **The ordering is load-bearing and is bounded on one side.** Core is **not** covered by that catch — `x402-bootstrap.php` is required directly by `App::boot()` — so a by-reference listener in core would throw out of boot and white-screen every request. The listener was therefore converted **before** the enforcement landed, and `HooksTest::testCoreItselfRegistersNoByReferenceListener` scans `installer/core/` and fails if one ever reappears. That test is a safety interlock, not a style check, and its docblock says so.
- **Registration-time, not dispatch-time, and the cost decides it.** Shipped code holds **23** action registrations and **32** filter registrations — **55** reflections, once, at boot — against **363** action fire sites and **128** filter fire sites, many of them inside loops. Checking at dispatch would cost a reflection per callback per fire, on every request, forever.
- **The filter path is closed in the same move rather than left as the next NEW-03.** `applyFilters()` spreads its arguments into `call_user_func()`, so a `&$value` filter callback loses its write exactly as an action does. **Zero code relies on it today** (verified two ways), so the check is shared by one private helper consumed by both registries — one implementation, per the reuse rule.
- **By-reference CAPTURE (`use ( &$x )`) is unaffected**, works correctly, and is used by four existing tests. Reflection reports parameters only. Pinned by a named test, because a check that conflated the two would have refused all four and looked like a broken suite rather than a broken check.
- **A SECOND, independent defect was found by driving the feature instead of the finding (L-014), and fixed in path.** NEW-03 says the x402 post-type default "is never applied". It could never have applied even with a working hook: `admin/post-type-edit.php:73` applies `admin.post_type_edit.update_data` — the filter x402 uses to add `x402_default_enabled` — and passes the result to `PostTypeManager::update()`, whose **hardcoded 7-field allow-list silently drops it**. So the checkbox x402 renders on the post-type edit form (`x402-bootstrap.php:167`) has **never persisted**: the operator ticks it, saves, and it returns unticked. Six code sites read the value; nothing could write it. Recorded as **NEW-36**. Fixed in path because acceptance criterion 1 of this sprint is unreachable without it — the NEW-16 shape from slice 7, "in scope by necessity, not opportunism" (D-043).
- **The fix for that is an extension point, not a longer hardcoded list:** `$updatable` now passes through a new **`post_type.updatable_fields`** filter, and x402 declares its own two keys. **Omission still denies**, so no plugin widens it into mass assignment by accident — the `admin.gate_map` (D-032) / `mcp.tool_capabilities` (D-048) shape, chosen over adding `x402_default_enabled` to core's own list, which would hardcode a plugin's concern into the post-type manager.
- **`phpunit.xml` sets `failOnWarning="true"`, which the file itself named as this slice's trigger.** It was off *because* of NEW-03. Leaving it off after the fix would have discarded the evidence that proves the fix: the warning's **absence** is the user-facing outcome, and it is now asserted across all 220 tests rather than only where a test looks for it.
- **Corrections of record, measured rather than inherited (L-015).** D-026, audit NEW-03 and audit D-04 all say `doAction()` "backs **301 registered actions**". That figure does not reproduce and is wrong in kind: it is a stale copy of `docs/api/INDEX.md`'s `| Actions |` row **at commit `622d54c`**, traced through that file's history (301 → 302 → 304 → 306 → 307 → 308 at HEAD). Nothing *registers* 301 anything. Re-measured **three** ways, the last of them comment-stripped and multi-line-aware: **308** distinct action names, **363** action fire sites, **23** shipped action registrations, **120** distinct filter names, **128** filter fire sites, **32** shipped filter registrations, max **4** payload arguments. The blast radius of the by-reference defect is **one listener** — and the inflated framing is part of why it was deferred twice.
- **My own first two measurements of that surface disagreed with each other, and reconciling them found a real gap — recorded because it is L-015 turned on this very entry.** A line-based grep and a subagent's count differed on registrations (35 vs 27) and filter names (115 vs 118). Neither was wrong so much as differently scoped: one included `tests/` (where this slice's own new file added 9 registrations), the other counted the helper's own delegation line as a registration, and both missed the **4 multi-line `applyFilters()` calls** a line-based pattern cannot see. The third pass — strip comments, strip the helper definitions, match across newlines — settled it, and in doing so found that **`x402.should_protect` (`x402/gate.php:70`) was fired in code with no `docs/api/INDEX.md` row**, a pre-existing gap dating to adoption's line-based extraction. Row added; Filters **119 → 120**, total **961**. Had the numbers not been re-derived, this entry would have shipped a figure copied from a subagent report while arguing that copied figures are not measurements. Also corrected: audit NEW-03 cited `core/app.php:486` for the unconditional x402 load; the real line is `:546`, now named by method so the next insertion cannot rot it.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **`doActionRefArray()`**, the WordPress `do_action_ref_array` shape (probed, works — rejected because it creates a *second* dispatch contract, so "may this action mutate?" depends on which method the emitter happened to call, invisible to a listener author; that is the by-omission shape S-07, NEW-02 and the tool loader were each closed to eliminate, and it would force enforcement to per-dispatch reflection); **`mixed &...$args`** (refuted above by measurement); **log and refuse to register** (a listener silently absent is the defect, not the fix); **detect nothing and fix only x402** (leaves the next by-reference listener on any of 308 names silently discarding its writes — "fix one of two identical paths", which D-041's own review cycle caught and rejected); **a `keel-verify` check for by-reference listeners** (the runtime enforcement is universal and cannot be bypassed; a static source check would duplicate it and could disagree — its absence is stated in the script header, the D-045 precedent); **adding x402's keys to core's allow-list** (hardcodes a plugin concern into core and leaves the extension point still broken for everyone else).
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **NEW-03**; opens and closes **NEW-36** in the same slice. Discharges D-026's deferral of NEW-03 at its own recorded trigger; D-026's NEW-04 half is untouched.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-25, before commit — L-015): both subagents ran on the finished diff, docs included, and NEITHER returned a blocking finding.** Recorded rather than folded in silently, because the two reviewers **disagreed with each other** on one item and most of the rest were defects in my own text.
  - **They disagreed about `post_type.updatable_fields`, and the auditor was right.** The `code-reviewer` called the absence of a reserved-key denylist a real gap, on the grounds that `list()` and `getMenuItems()` "trust `$pt['builtin']` as authoritative". The `security-auditor` returned **no finding** on the same code, having traced that the storage key is the `$id` **parameter** and never `$postType['id']`, and that `delete()`'s protection compares `$id === self::BUILTIN_PAGE` rather than reading the record. Re-derived myself rather than picking a side (L-013): `$pt['builtin']` is read in exactly two places, `:232-233` (sort order) and `:1571` (menu display). **Both cosmetic. The auditor is right and the reviewer overstated it.** The `array_diff( $updatable, [ 'id', 'builtin', 'created_at' ] )` guard is kept anyway and is described honestly as **defence in depth, not a vulnerability fix**: `id` diverging from its own storage key is an integrity hazard whatever currently reads it, and a field that is cosmetic today is one requirement away from not being. Pinned by a test proven to fail against the un-guarded code, with a positive control in the same test so it cannot pass by the filter simply not running.
  - **The `security-auditor` found the interlock test was scanning the wrong half of the risk, and that is the finding of the cycle.** `testCoreItselfRegistersNoByReferenceListener` scanned `installer/core/` only. But `installer/admin/bootstrap.php` registers **five** hooks at top level, is required by every admin page and endpoint, and sits outside PluginLoader's catch **and** outside its own two try/catch blocks, which close well above those registrations — and **no test boots it at all**, because `IntegrationTestCase` calls `App::boot()` directly. So a by-reference listener added there would have been caught by nothing until a real admin request hit it, which is strictly worse than the core case the test was written for. The scan now covers `installer/core/` **and** `installer/admin/`, proven by planting an offender in `admin/` (the old scan missed it entirely) and watching it go quiet on removal.
  - **The `code-reviewer` found the scan's own comment was false.** It claimed to "over-match rather than under-match"; it could in fact under-match — a direct `Hooks::addAction()` call, an arrow function, or a closure assigned to a variable first. The pattern now matches both spellings and both function forms, **proven against nine cases** (six that must match including multi-line signatures, three that must not, including `use ( &$x )` capture). The remaining gap — a callback assigned to a variable before registration — is now **stated in the docblock** rather than denied, together with why it is acceptable: this is the second line of defence, and `refuseByReferenceCallback()` catches every spelling at runtime.
  - **A check that scanned nothing would have reported PASS.** Added a floor assertion on the file count with the message *"it has not passed, it did not run"* — keel-verify's placeholder check shipped with exactly this hole (L-016).
  - **The exception message carried an ABSOLUTE path, breaking this file's own neighbouring convention.** The message travels: a plugin tripping the refusal fails to load, and `PluginLoader::loadErrors` surfaces the text on the admin plugins page and through `klytos_list_plugins`. Both are gated at `plugins.manage`, so no boundary is crossed — but `plugin-loader.php:220` deliberately uses `basename()` for its sibling error two lines from where this one lands. The message now carries the basename; the absolute path stays available, structured, through `getCallbackLocation()`.
  - **`page.save_data` runs after sanitization and nothing re-checks it** — `buildPageData()` sanitizes `content_html` and validates `status` *before* the filter, and the returned value is written straight to storage. The auditor traced every caller and confirmed **no unauthenticated or lower-privileged path was newly exposed** (`autosave.php` at `pages.edit`; the MCP tools at `pages.create`/`pages.edit`; nothing in `installer/public/`), and that a plugin already has equivalent power through `StorageInterface::write()`. So it is not a new trust boundary — but `docs/reference/hooks.md` now says outright that a filter touching `content_html` must re-run `klytos_kses_post()`, that `status` must not become scheduled without a valid `publish_at`, and that `slug` is the storage key. Writing "modify freely" would have been the L-002 defect in a document written to prevent it.
  - **One finding was STALE rather than wrong** — the auditor reported the `post_types`/`post-types` collection typo in the test's `tearDown`, which had already been fixed from the `code-reviewer`'s earlier report of the same line. L-015's other half: an accusation can be true of the moment it was read and false of the moment it lands.
  - **Both reviewers again ran without a shell** and said so unprompted. The limit is recorded rather than glossed: neither could execute `phpunit`, `phpcs` or `git diff`, so every count in this decision rests on measurements taken in the main session. Where they could verify by reading — the reflection logic across all callable shapes, `use ( &$x )` being unaffected, the filter/action ordering in both `PageManager` methods, the x402 early-return semantics, the absence of any first-party `set_exception_handler()` — they did, and those conclusions are theirs.

## D-055 — Owner recovery is a CLI command that works without a session; refusals exit non-zero
- Date / phase: 2026-07-25 / Phase 5, Sprint 4 slice 2 (the sprint scope and the NEW-33 boundary were user decisions taken before anything was written)
- Decision: `owner:repair --email=<address>` is added to `TerminalExecutor`, declaring `users.manage`. It writes the missing `admin_email` into config and then runs the product's **own** `UserManager::migrateFromV1Config()`, which rebuilds the owner record from `config['admin_user']` and `config['admin_pass_hash']`. The operator's existing password still applies; the command sets no credentials. It refuses — naming the existing owner — when one already exists, refuses with its usage line when `--email` is missing or invalid, and refuses when config retains no admin credentials to restore from. Closes audit **NEW-08**. *(This is the CORRECTED design; the first implementation took `--username`/`--password` and was refuted in review — see the amendment at the end of this entry.)*
- **Why the CLI, and why the permission looks like it does nothing.** Recovery must work with **no session**, which rules out the admin panel by construction — that is the whole defect. Verified against source rather than assumed: `TerminalExecutor::dispatch()` performs **no** permission check (the checks live only in the web wrapper at `:225-229`), and `installer/cli.php` calls `dispatch()` directly. That asymmetry is deliberate and pre-existing — reaching the CLI already means holding filesystem access to the installation, which is strictly more power than any account has. The declared `users.manage` is what gates the command in the **web** terminal, where a session exists and a role is meaningful. So the permission is not decoration and it is not a hole; it governs the one transport where it can.
- **Refusals THROW rather than return, and that is a correctness property, not a style choice.** `dispatch()` reports `success => true` for any handler that returns without throwing, and `cli.php` derives its **exit code** from that field. A refusal returned as a string would therefore have exited **0** — telling an automated recovery script that a repair which changed nothing had succeeded. Found by a test asserting `success === false`, not by reading.
- **Uniqueness stays structural.** `migrateFromV1Config()` returns the existing owner untouched when one is present, so the command cannot mint a second. The `findOwner()` check in the command is not a second guard — it exists so the refusal can **name** the current owner, which is the one fact an operator locked out of an install needs.
- **Two of the five tests initially passed against the unfixed tree for the WRONG reason**, and that is the transferable part: an unknown command also makes `dispatch()` report failure, so `assertFalse( $result['success'] )` held before the command existed. Tightened to assert the refusal's **reason** — the message must name the existing owner, or name the missing flag, and must NOT be the "unknown command" text. **L-012's rule** (assert the reason, not just the outcome) applied to a command surface.
- **The recreated owner is proven to AUTHENTICATE, not merely to exist.** Recreating a record is not the same as restoring access — if the password were stored wrong the record would look perfect and the operator would still be locked out. The test authenticates with the right password and asserts the wrong one fails, so the positive control cannot pass vacuously (L-014, L-010).
- **NEW-33 is deliberately NOT closed here, by explicit user decision.** This slice touches `terminal-executor.php`, which fires NEW-33's recorded trigger ("the next slice touching `terminal-executor.php`"). The file carries **14** hardcoded Spanish strings against D-006's English-base rule. Converting them means ~14 keys × 20 locales, each of which must satisfy the orthography contract, and that is a coherent i18n slice with its own test point rather than a rider on a recovery command — the tangling D-025, D-026, D-029 and D-031 each refused in turn. The trigger is re-bound to **the next slice touching that file, or a dedicated i18n slice**, whichever comes first.
- **The visible cost of that boundary, stated rather than discovered later:** this command's four strings ARE English catalogue keys in all 20 locales (D-006 is not optional for new strings), so `help` now lists 25 Spanish command descriptions and one translated one. A real, temporary inconsistency that NEW-33's closure removes. Recording it so the next reader does not file it as a fresh defect.
- **`__()` resolves on this path — proven, not assumed.** NEW-18 records that the *global* `__()` is declared only under `admin/`, while `app.php` declares a **namespaced** one; `terminal-executor.php` is in `Klytos\Core`, so resolution follows the namespace of the file where the call is written (the D-053 finding). Proven by the tests asserting on `--email` and on the substituted username, neither of which appears in the catalogue KEYS — so a miss, which returns the key verbatim, would have failed them.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **an admin page** (cannot work — the state it recovers from is precisely one where no login can succeed); **repair during boot, automatically** (silently creating an owner with generated credentials on a damaged install is a privilege-granting side effect nobody asked for, and it would mask the underlying config fault D-031 deliberately made visible); **also resetting an existing owner's password** (a different operation with different consequences, and the command would then need to distinguish "recover" from "take over" — left unbuilt and named in the reference doc); **a `--force` flag to replace an existing owner** (rejected outright: an unauthenticated CLI path that can replace the owner of a working install is a worse primitive than the problem).
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **NEW-08**; discharges the half of D-031 that was left explicitly open ("It does not restore access").
- **AMENDMENT (2026-07-25, before commit — the slice's own `code-reviewer` returned a BLOCKING finding that was correct and redesigned this command).** Recorded in full because the defect is the exact one this project keeps writing lessons about.
  - **The first implementation created a record nobody could log in as.** It took `--username`, `--email` and `--password`, and called `UserManager::create()`. But `Auth::login()` — the admin panel's real gate — validates the username against `config['admin_user']` and the password against **`config['admin_pass_hash']`**, never against the user record (that is NEW-11, which this slice's own reference doc had already written down three paragraphs below its headline claim). So the supplied password could never log anyone in; and once `findOwner()` returned non-null, the command **refused to run again**, leaving the install permanently unrecoverable through the product. The reviewer's phrase for it is exact: verified against the failure mode its author modelled, not against the end-to-end path it exists to restore.
  - **My own test hid it, and it is the test I had written invoking L-014.** `testTheRecreatedOwnerCanActuallyAuthenticate` asserted `UserManager::authenticate()` — the manager — not `Auth::login()` — the gate. It passed against a command that restored nothing. Writing "recreating a record is not the same as restoring access" in a docblock and then testing the wrong layer is L-014 quoted and disobeyed in the same file.
  - **The corrected design follows from what actually breaks** (verified: `upgrade-assert.php:131` removes **only** `admin_email`; `admin_user` and `admin_pass_hash` survive). The missing piece is the **email**, not the identity — so the command supplies it and runs the existing migration. That also satisfies the no-duplication rule: there is now still exactly one code path that creates an owner from config, rather than a second one beside it. **User decision**, taken on the corrected shape before it was written.
  - **The alternative was rejected on security grounds, not effort:** also writing `config['admin_pass_hash']` would make the supplied password real and would recover an install whose config password is lost too — at the cost of an unauthenticated CLI primitive that resets the owner password on any install. If that is ever wanted it belongs in a separately-named command, not hidden inside "repair".
  - **The test now drives the REAL gate**, `Auth::login()`, with the wrong password asserted first (it returns before touching the session, so that half needs no session and cannot be the only thing observed) and the right one second, under a real `session_start()` — the one place this tier deliberately deviates from its own design, stated in the test rather than left to be discovered.
  - **D-039's config guard fired on the new tests and was right.** The repair writes `config.json.enc`, and `App::$config` is decrypted once at boot with no invalidation path, so a later test would read a stale value and pass for the wrong reason. `#[RunInSeparateProcess]` did **not** silence it (the guard detects the mutation, not the staleness). Resolved by having the tests pass the email config **already holds**, making the write net-zero: the command still takes the same branch, still writes the file, still runs the migration, and the bytes come back identical. Suppressing the guard for this file was rejected — the whole tier depends on it.
  - **The `security-auditor` returned a separate BLOCKING finding on the FIRST design, and its fix was kept even though the redesign made it moot.** `execute()` persisted the typed command into the `terminal` storage collection — **absent from `ENCRYPTED_PATHS` at every level**, so plaintext on disk — and into the audit log, and echoed it back to the browser. `admin/logs.php` is gated at `site.configure`, which resolves to owner **and admin**, so an admin could have read the owner's password out of the log. Verified link by link. The command no longer takes a password, so the finding no longer applies to it — but the weakness is real and now known, so a `redactSecrets()` pass was added in `execute()`, at the one place every command's text is persisted, masking `--password`/`--secret`/`--token`/`--key` values before history, log or response see them. Fixing the mechanism rather than the one command is the S-07 inversion; a future command taking a `--token` is safe without its author remembering anything.
  - **Recorded and NOT fixed**, each verified: a bare `--username` (no `=value`) becomes the literal string `'true'` in `cli.php`'s parser — pre-existing, shared by every command, and no longer reachable here since the command takes no username; `findOwner()` and the migration's write are not atomic, so two concurrent repairs could race (same trust boundary as the filesystem access the CLI already assumes — now stated in the reference doc); and the password-as-a-flag exposure in shell history and `ps`, which the redesign removes entirely by not taking one.
  - **Confirmed sound by both reviewers against source:** the exit-code mapping (throw → `success:false` → exit 1; return → exit 0) with no no-op-exits-0 path; `UserManager::create()`'s bcrypt cost-12 hashing and single `LOCK_EX` write; the refusal-reason assertions correctly guarding the "unknown command also fails" trap (L-012); playground cleanliness under D-030/D-039/D-042; and all four — now five — catalogue keys structurally identical across the 20 locales with `{username}` spelled the same and the orthography correct in every accented language, checked individually.

## D-056 — The user RECORD is the sole login authority; the v1 config credential survives only as the seed
- Date / phase: 2026-07-25 / Phase 5, Sprint 5 kickoff (user decision, taken before anything was written)
- Decision: `Auth::login()` (`installer/core/auth.php:83`) stops verifying credentials against `config['admin_user']` / `config['admin_pass_hash']` and delegates to the already-implemented `UserManager::authenticate()` (`user-manager.php:384`). After this slice there is **exactly one** authority for "is this username and password valid": the user record. `config['admin_user']` and `config['admin_pass_hash']` remain in config, but only in the role they already have elsewhere in the product — the **seed** that `UserManager::migrateFromV1Config()` and `owner:repair` (D-055) rebuild a missing owner record *from*. Nothing reads them to grant access again.
- **Why a fallback was rejected, and this is the load-bearing part.** The obvious shape is record-first, config-fallback: try `UserManager::authenticate()`, and if it fails, try the old config comparison. It looks strictly safer for an installed base and it is not, for a measured reason. **Verified live this session with a net-zero probe** (the password was changed and restored inside one run):
  ```
  UserManager::changePassword( owner, NEW )
  UserManager::authenticate( owner, NEW ) -> ACCEPTED   the record updated
  Auth::login( owner, NEW )               -> REFUSED    the change never reached the gate
  Auth::login( owner, OLD )               -> ACCEPTED   the old password still works
  ```
  The two authorities have **already diverged in the product** — `changePassword()` (`user-manager.php:424`) writes `pass_hash` on the record and nothing writes `config['admin_pass_hash']` — so a config fallback does not preserve a working path, it preserves the **defect**: the old password would go on being accepted forever, in the branch nobody looks at, on the only account that can log in. Recorded separately as audit **NEW-37**.
- **What the installed base actually depends on, checked rather than assumed:** `App::boot()` Step 10b runs `migrateFromV1Config()` idempotently (D-021/D-031), which builds the owner record **from those same config values**, so an upgraded v1 install already carries a record whose `pass_hash` is the operator's current password. The upgrade path is therefore not a new dependency introduced here — it is the dependency D-021 already built and `scripts/dev/upgrade-test.sh` already exercises from the real v0.30.1. That test becomes load-bearing in a new way this sprint: it now proves *login*, not merely *boot*.
- **The residual risk, stated plainly rather than discovered later:** an install whose owner record is missing loses login entirely, where today it logs in and is then denied everywhere by the gate. That is a narrower failure than it sounds — the boot migration recreates the record, and `owner:repair --email=` (D-055) restores it from the CLI with no session, which is exactly the state it was built for. The two sprints compose; the upgrade test is what proves they do.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **record-first with config fallback** (no upgrade risk at all, and it keeps two authorities forever — the rotation defect survives inside the fallback branch, and a defect that only fires in a fallback is the hardest kind to ever remove); **config-first with record fallback** (the current behaviour plus a widening — the stale config password would keep winning, which is the exact bug); **migrate `admin_pass_hash` on every password change so both stay in sync** (preserves both authorities honestly, but it writes a bcrypt hash of the live credential into core config for every account, and S-04's whole lesson is that two copies of one decision drift — here they would drift into an *authentication* bypass).
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **NEW-11** and, in the same slice, **NEW-37**. Depends on D-021's boot migration and composes with D-055.
- **Implementation note 1 (slice 1) — the application-password credential now fails one layer EARLIER, which is a spec correction and not a test fix.** `validateAppPassword()` used to accept any credential whose username equalled `config['admin_user']`; it now requires the username to resolve to an **active** user record. The existing `McpActorResolutionTest::testAValidCredentialWhoseUserIsGoneResolvesToNoActor` pinned the old mechanism in its *precondition* — *"validateAppPassword() still accepts the credential; the store just can no longer attribute the caller to a role"* — so it went red the moment the delegation landed. The **property** that test exists for is unchanged and is now satisfied more strictly: a credential the store cannot attribute **denies**, and it denies at authentication (HTTP 401) instead of at the authorization gate (HTTP 403, via D-046's null-role deny). Both fail closed; the layer moved. The test was **corrected, never weakened** — it now asserts `validate()` is false AND `getActor()` is null, so it still fails if an orphan credential were ever to authenticate, and it fails in a second way if one were ever to carry an actor. Recorded here first, then changed, per Phase 5's "a wrong test is a spec correction".
- **Implementation note 2 (slice 1) — the lockout is keyed by the SUBMITTED username, not by the resolved account, and that is a deliberate departure from the approved plan.** The plan said "key it per resolved account, with one shared bucket for usernames that do not resolve", and argued no existence oracle appears because both cases answer with the same strings. **The strings are not where the oracle would be.** With a shared bucket for unresolvable names, an attacker fills it with garbage and then reads the *bucket* — a target that answers `login_failed` has its own bucket and therefore exists; one that answers `account_locked` shares the garbage bucket and therefore does not. Keying on `hash( 'sha256', $username )` removes the distinction entirely: every submitted string gets its own bucket whether or not it resolves, and no resolution happens before the lockout check at all. L-014's rule decided it — the acceptance criterion ("no existence oracle") is the requirement, the plan's shape was a guess. Case is deliberately not normalised, because `authenticate()` compares usernames exactly, so attempts under another spelling can never succeed anyway.
- **Implementation note 3 (slice 1) — one file, pruned on write, rather than one file per account.** The old implementation wrote `sys_get_temp_dir() . '/klytos_lockout_' . md5( … ) . '.json'`, one file per key. Per-username keying would have turned that into unbounded file creation from invented usernames — the **NEW-19** shape (a limiter whose identifier set grows monotonically), reproduced in a second subsystem. The map lives in ONE file under the install's `data/` directory, reached through `StorageInterface::getDataDir()` (already on the interface, already used by `ActionScheduler` and `CronManager`, so no new dependency and no App-singleton coupling), and expired entries are dropped on every write. **`MCP\RateLimiter` was assessed for reuse and rejected on the record**, per the reuse rule: its window is a hardcoded 60 seconds and its auth-failure threshold a hardcoded 10, both IP-keyed — expressing "5 attempts, 15-minute lockout, per account" through it would mean changing those constants for the MCP surface too, i.e. weakening a shipped control to avoid writing thirty lines. The 5/15 semantics and the `account_locked:{minutes}` contract the login form renders are preserved exactly; only the key and the location change.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-25, before commit — L-015): both subagents ran on the finished diff, docs included. The `code-reviewer` returned ONE BLOCKING finding, it was correct, and it was my own guard defeating itself.** Recorded in full because the shape is the transferable part.
  - **BLOCKING, and right: the owner-suspend guard was bypassable in a single call.** `update()` iterates `$updatable` in order — `role` sits **before** `status` — and assigns `$user[$field] = $data[$field]` inside the same loop. So `update( $ownerId, [ 'role' => 'admin', 'status' => 'suspended' ] )` had already overwritten `$user['role']` with `'admin'` by the time my guard read it: the owner was demoted **and** suspended in one call, straight through the check written to prevent exactly that. Reachable owner-only, including over MCP through `klytos_update_user`, which passes both fields as independent parameters. **Re-derived against source before acting** (L-013/L-015) rather than taken on the reviewer's word — it reproduces exactly as described. Fixed by comparing against `$oldRole`, which the method already captures at the top for the role-change hook; pinned by `testTheOwnerCannotBeSuspendedByDemotingInTheSameCall`, proven to fail against the pre-fix tree.
  - **A second guard was added in the same place, from the auditor's finding 5:** nothing stopped an owner **demoting themselves**, leaving an install with **no owner** — the state D-031 contains and D-055 exists to repair. `update()` now refuses to remove the owner role, mirroring its own pre-existing refusal to grant it, so `transferOwnership()` is the single supported path and the "exactly one owner" invariant this class documents at the top of its file holds at every point.
  - **The honest nuance nobody had checked:** the combined-call bypass produced a **recoverable** install (zero owners → `findOwner()` null → `owner:repair` proceeds), while the single-field suspend my guard blocks produces the **unrecoverable** one (a suspended owner exists → `owner:repair` refuses). The guard was therefore covering the worse case by luck rather than by design, and it is now correct in both spellings.
  - **NEW-39 — the login form was an account-status oracle by TIMING, and it is fixed in path.** `authenticate()` returned early for an unknown username and for a non-active account, so only *"exists and is active"* reached `password_verify()`. **Measured before fixing** (median of 12): **218.98 ms** for an active account with a wrong password against **0.65 ms** suspended and **0.64 ms** non-existent — 340×, one probe per candidate, and the per-account lockout does not mitigate it because enumeration never needs five tries. Pre-existing code, but the exposure is this slice's: until D-056 the method was only reachable from re-authentication behind an existing session. It also made this slice's own new reference doc assert "Nothing in the response distinguishes them", which is the **L-002** defect. Every outcome now performs one bcrypt verify, against another stored record's hash — a real hash rather than a committed literal, so the cost matches exactly, no bcrypt string enters the repository, and there is no first-call-in-the-process outlier, which under `php -S` would have **inverted** the oracle rather than closing it. Re-measured after: **217.55 / 219.13 / 218.05 ms**.
  - **The two reviewers described NEW-39 differently and one of them was wrong — L-023 again.** The `code-reviewer` framed it as "no bcrypt when the username matches no record", which omits the suspended case entirely; the `security-auditor` framed it as *active* versus *suspended-or-nonexistent*, which is what the code does. Settled by measuring rather than by preferring the more plausible report — and the measurement is what turned a plausible-sounding note into a 340× number worth acting on.
  - **Recorded and NOT fixed, each with a trigger: NEW-40** (the lockout's read-modify-write is not atomic and nothing throttles the login endpoint — the NEW-20 shape in a second subsystem, with `ActionScheduler::acquireLock()` named as the fix shape) and **NEW-41** (a suspended user's OAuth access token keeps working for up to an hour — the one credential type where suspension does not take effect, now stated in `authentication.md`'s suspension table rather than implied away).
  - **Three smaller corrections, all mine:** the `$userManager` docblock cited "Step 10a" for Auth's construction when `app.php` creates it at **Step 7** (the ordering claim was right, the label was invented); `mcp-authorization.md`'s credential table still read *"pinned to the admin user"* for application passwords, four lines above the prose explaining that D-056 removed the pin; and the new `upgrade-assert.php` checks were worded so that a **passing** run printed *"the upgraded owner CANNOT LOG IN"* — `check()` prints its message on PASS, so the first draft asserted falsehoods on success, the L-002 defect in a test's own output.
  - **Confirmed sound by both reviewers against source, not merely read:** no remaining path authenticates against `config['admin_user']`/`admin_pass_hash` (every surviving hit is the seed — `migrateFromV1Config`, `owner:repair`, `install.php` — or a comment describing it); `resolveUserId()` is fully deleted with zero remaining references; every caller of `login()` and `validateAppPassword()` was enumerated and is consistent with the new authority; the lockout key cannot reach a filename or a JSON key (it is hashed); `data/` is not served over HTTP; usernames cannot be changed after creation, so a credential cannot be re-pointed at another account; and every new test asserts through `Auth::login()` rather than the manager (L-024 respected).
  - **Both reviewers ran without a shell and said so unprompted.** The limit is recorded rather than glossed: neither could execute `git diff`, `phpunit` or a timing measurement, so their conclusions rest on reading, and every number in this amendment was measured in the main session.
- **Implementation note 4 (slice 1) — a private near-duplicate was deleted, and NEW-29 was fixed in path.** `Auth::resolveUserId()` was a second implementation of `UserManager::getByUsername()`, and its only caller was the config comparison this decision removes (private, one call site, zero references in tests — verified repo-wide before deleting, per L-007). And `validateAppPassword()`'s `foreach ( $data['passwords'] ?? [] as &$stored )` — audit **NEW-29**, the L-017 footgun, which has silently discarded every `last_used` update the method ever made — is fixed here rather than deferred again: this slice rewrites that method, so D-031's narrowing applies to it exactly as it did to the four defects D-033 fixed in path.

## D-057 — Sprint 5 scope: NEW-09 rides along; NEW-26, NEW-32 and NEW-13 do not
- Date / phase: 2026-07-25 / Phase 5, Sprint 5 kickoff (user decision, taken before anything was written)
- Decision: Sprint 5 carries **NEW-11** (slice 1) and **NEW-09** (slice 2, passkey second-factor login). **NEW-26** (the password-reset form has no CSRF), **NEW-32** (the authorization audit hooks reach no sink) and **NEW-13** (the identity export has no re-auth, no 2FA and no owner notification) are **not** in scope, each keeps the trigger it already carries, and each is named as out of scope in `docs/sprints/sprint-5.md` rather than left implicit.
