- **Why NEW-09 belongs here and the other three do not.** NEW-09 is the same subsystem, and D-036 bound it to this slice by name — its fix shape is already written down, and its forbidden one-liner (`$preAuthScripts`) becomes *safe* only once slice 2's restrictions exist, so doing it anywhere else means either shipping a takeover primitive or re-deriving the whole analysis. The other three are adjacent surfaces that merely *touch* authentication: NEW-26 is a CSRF field on a form, NEW-32 is a logging design with real volume and content decisions (D-031's narrowing applies — it is a different subsystem), and NEW-13 adds re-authentication, a second-factor prompt and a mail dependency to the identity export, which D-040 already refused to fold into a slice for exactly this reason.
- **Why naming them explicitly is part of the decision, not bookkeeping:** an unnamed exclusion reads as an oversight to the next reader, and this project has twice had a reviewer re-raise something that was a recorded decision (`.cursor/agents/`, the inner-paren style). Naming them costs three lines and removes a whole class of re-litigation.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **NEW-11 alone, NEW-09 in its own sprint** (a smaller, cleaner sprint — but slice 1 makes 2FA reachable for three more roles, so shipping it while passkey login is known-broken widens a broken flow deliberately); **add NEW-26** (genuinely small, and it is on `reset-password.php`, a file slice 1 must already re-read — but the exploitability is low by its own recorded assessment and it is not required by any acceptance criterion here); **add NEW-32** (attractive because this sprint produces exactly the refusals nobody can currently read — and that is an argument for a logging slice, not for a rider on an authentication one).
- Supersedes: none. Bounds the sprint recorded in `docs/sprints/sprint-5.md`.

## D-058 — Passkey second-factor login is completed in the order that makes D-036's exemption safe, and the pre-auth list stops being keyed by basename
- Date / phase: 2026-07-25 / Phase 5, Sprint 5 slice 2
- Decision: audit **NEW-09** is closed in four steps, in this order and no other: **(1)** `admin/api/webauthn-challenge.php` restricts `register_challenge` and `register_complete` to **fully authenticated** callers, leaving only `auth_challenge` reachable while 2FA is pending; **(2)** `admin/login.php`'s 2FA dispatcher gains the `passkey` branch, wired to the already-complete `TwoFactor::verifyPasskeyAssertion()`; **(3)** enrolling an authenticator emails the account holder (`TwoFactor::sendPasskeyEnrolledEmail()`) and fires a new `user.passkey_enrolled` action; **(4)** **only then** does `api/webauthn-challenge.php` join `$preAuthScripts` in `admin/bootstrap.php`. Step 4 was the last line written in the slice.
- **Why the order is the decision and not a detail.** D-036 recorded that step 4 alone — the one-line fix this project has called FORBIDDEN for four sprints — is a full account-takeover primitive: `is2faPending()` is true after a correct **password alone**, and `completePasskeyRegistration()` enrols a credential and sets `enabled = true` **without checking any existing factor**. Verified against source again in this slice rather than taken from the record: both facts still hold exactly. Step 1 is what removes the primitive; every other step is inert without it. `PasskeyLoginTest::testRegistrationIsRefusedWhileTwoFactorIsMerelyPending` is therefore not a nice-to-have — it is the standing proof that step 4 is still safe, and it is written so it fails if step 1 is ever relaxed.
- **The D-043 question, asked before acting and answered in writing:** what does an ANONYMOUS caller now reach? Nothing new. `admin/bootstrap.php` runs the cron manager and the action scheduler **above** the auth guard, so they already execute for every anonymous request to `login.php`, `logout.php` and `reset-password.php`; a fourth entry adds no capability that the first three did not already offer. The endpoint's own check still answers **401** to a caller with neither a session nor a pending challenge. This is the question D-036 recorded as unasked the first time; asking it changed nothing here, which is itself the answer rather than a formality.
- **A second-order defect was found while adding the entry, and fixing it is part of this decision.** `$preAuthScripts` was matched against `basename( $_SERVER['SCRIPT_NAME'] )`. **Six** filenames exist in both `admin/` and `admin/api/` — `ai-chat`, `logs`, `plugins`, `tasks`, `terminal`, `translations` — which is precisely the collision **D-032** keyed the gate map by *path* to avoid, and this list had inherited the weaker scheme because until now every entry was top-level. Adding the first `api/` entry is what made it matter. The list now matches `klytos_admin_gate_key()` — the existing, tested helper, resolving from `SCRIPT_FILENAME` rather than the URL, returning **null** for anything outside `admin/`, which matches nothing and therefore requires authentication (fail-closed). Reused rather than reinvented, and nothing was exploitable: `webauthn-challenge.php` is unique repo-wide. The mechanism was one same-named file away from being.
- **`Helpers::webauthnRpId()` exists so the rpId is derived once.** A passkey is bound to the Relying Party ID it was registered under, so registration and verification must derive it identically; a one-character divergence invalidates every stored credential and presents as a broken authenticator rather than a broken string. The endpoint and the login page now read the same function. The port is stripped because the spec's rpId is a domain, never an origin — `localhost:8083` is not a valid rpId, which is the case every local playground hits.
- **The notification is an action AND a mail, and neither can fail the enrolment.** The credential is already stored by the time both run, so a mail failure must not report an error the storage layer disagrees with; `sendPasskeyEnrolledEmail()` catches and logs. Nothing in core subscribes to `user.passkey_enrolled`, and `docs/reference/authentication.md` says so in those words — **L-019** was recorded because "it goes to the audit log" was true of a hook and false of the system.
- **Proven without a browser, and that is the part worth keeping.** The tests generate a P-256 key with `openssl_pkey_new`, hand-encode the COSE public key and the CBOR attestation object, and produce a real ES256 signature over `authData || SHA-256( clientDataJSON )`. The credential is enrolled through the product's **own** `completePasskeyRegistration()` rather than written into storage (**L-005**: a fixture that bypasses the application proves only that the fixture works), so registration and login are proven to compose. A tampered-signature test exists so the passing case cannot be passing because the dispatcher accepts anything assertion-shaped. All four were observed FAILING before step 4 landed (L-016).
- **Correction of record:** audit NEW-09 says the endpoint "gates all **four** of its actions" on the weak condition. There are **three** plus an unknown-action branch. Re-counted against source (L-015); the substance is unchanged, since two of the three were the dangerous pair.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **step 4 alone** (the forbidden one-liner — it is the takeover primitive, and it buys nothing besides, because passkey login could not complete regardless); **requiring an existing second factor before enrolment** (correct, and it is step-up authentication — a different subsystem with its own test point, recorded as **NEW-13** and explicitly out of scope per D-057; the enrolment email is the compensating control and is named as such rather than sold as equivalent); **keeping the basename match and adding `'webauthn-challenge.php'`** (smallest diff, works today, and leaves a list where a second file of the same name is silently exempt — the by-omission shape S-07, NEW-02 and the tool loader were each closed to eliminate); **a browser-driven end-to-end test** (proves the real UI, needs a virtual authenticator and a browser in CI, and would not have caught anything these tests miss on the server side).
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **NEW-09**; discharges **D-036**'s deferral at the trigger D-036 itself named.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-25, before commit — L-015): BOTH subagents returned a BLOCKING finding, both were correct, and the `security-auditor`'s is the most valuable single finding of this sprint — it proved this slice had NOT closed NEW-09 at all.**
  - **BLOCKING 1 — the feature did not work in a browser, and the test suite was green because the harness was fixing the product.** `login.php`'s and `security.php`'s `fetch()` calls send `Content-Type: application/json` and put the CSRF token INSIDE the JSON body. `Helpers::verifyCsrf()` reads `$_POST['csrf']`, `$_SERVER['HTTP_X_CSRF_TOKEN']` and `$_GET['csrf']` — and **PHP does not populate `$_POST` for a JSON body**, so the token was invisible and the endpoint answered **403** to every real browser call. Passkey registration AND passkey login were therefore unreachable for any actual user, on top of the two defects NEW-09 already named. Every test passed because `AdminHttpTestCase::postJson()` adds an `X-CSRF-Token` header — **a header the shipped page never sends** — and its own docblock explains why, i.e. the harness had the diagnosis written into it as a workaround while the product carried the bug. **Reproduced by running, not by reading**: a request built with exactly the shipped page's headers returned `403 {"error":"Invalid CSRF token"}`. This is **L-016's shape one turn further out** — not a measurement that goes green without observing anything, but a harness that quietly repairs the thing it is measuring.
  - The tell had been sitting in the file the whole time: `$csrf = $input['csrf'] ?? '';` was read into a variable and **never used**. It is used now. Fixed on both sides deliberately rather than either: the endpoint accepts the body token (via the existing `Auth::validateCsrf()`) **and** all three `fetch()` calls now send `X-CSRF-Token` like every sibling admin page — so neither a future JS change nor a future helper change can silently break it again. Pinned by `testTheEndpointAcceptsTheTokenTheShippedPageActuallySends`, which builds its request by hand *without* the header, is the only test here that reproduces the shipped page byte for byte, and was **observed failing first**.
  - **This decision's own "alternatives rejected" was wrong and is corrected rather than quietly dropped.** It said a browser-driven end-to-end test "would not have caught anything these tests miss on the server side". That is false: any faithful client — a browser, or the hand-built request now in the suite — catches this immediately. The claim was reasoning about the transport instead of exercising it.
  - **BLOCKING 2 (`code-reviewer`), also correct: the enrolment notification could report a stored credential as a failure.** `klytos_do_action( 'user.passkey_enrolled', … )` sat inside the same `try { } catch ( \RuntimeException )` as `completePasskeyRegistration()`, and `Hooks::doAction()` has no per-listener guard — so a plugin throwing `RuntimeException` would have produced `{"success": false}` and HTTP 400 for an enrolment that had already been persisted, and anything else it threw would have escaped uncaught. The comment beside it claimed "neither can fail the enrolment", which was true of the mail (it catches internally) and false of the action — this decision's own text, contradicted by its own code. The success response is now sent **before** the notification block, and that block has its own `catch ( \Throwable )`.
  - **Recorded and NOT fixed, each verified against source:** `verifyPasskeyAssertion()` stores the new signature count without comparing it to the stored one, so a cloned authenticator is not detected; it performs no `origin` check although its registration sibling does; and it reads `authData` offsets without a length guard, so a 32-byte `authenticatorData` produces an "uninitialized string offset" warning before failing closed. Also: the setup-wizard skip-list in `bootstrap.php` still matches by basename and was not extended with this endpoint, so during an incomplete setup the endpoint 302s to the wizard instead of answering JSON. All four go to the audit as **NEW-42** rather than being fixed in a slice already carrying two blocking corrections.
  - **One reviewer claim was REFUTED and is recorded as such:** the `security-auditor` was told L-016 documents a case-sensitivity defect and correctly pointed out that it does not — L-016 is the three-false-measurements lesson; the case-sensitivity defect is recorded in **D-045**'s review amendment. The brief was wrong, the reviewer was right, and it checked the underlying question anyway.
  - **Confirmed sound by both reviewers against source:** the takeover primitive is genuinely gone (the registration guard runs before `$userId` is even resolved, and `session_regenerate_id( true )` defeats fixation across the password stage); no user-substitution path exists, since `$userId` is always session-derived; the `$preAuthScripts` key change exempts exactly one new surface and nothing else, with traversal, symlinks and null bytes failing closed through `realpath()`; the CBOR/COSE fixture was checked byte-offset by byte-offset against the parser and genuinely matches it; the tampered-signature test is a real negative control; `Helpers::webauthnRpId()` has exactly two call sites and no other rpId derivation exists; and the two new catalogue keys are present in all 20 locales.
  - **Both reviewers again ran without a shell**, so the CSRF finding was *reported* from reading and *proven* in the main session by executing the request.

## D-059 — The counters become atomic within themselves, not under one wide lock; a lock that cannot be taken refuses the attempt
- Date / phase: 2026-07-26 / Phase 5, Sprint 6 kickoff (the fail direction and the scope of the two in-path findings were user decisions, taken before anything was written)
- Decision: **(a)** A new shared primitive at `installer/core/file-lock.php` holds a single `LOCK_EX` across read → decide → write, with a bounded retry deadline and an explicit fail-closed return. **(b)** `Auth::recordFailedAttempt()` / `resetLoginAttempts()` and `MCP\RateLimiter::check()` / `recordAuthFailure()` each run their whole read-modify-write inside it, closing **NEW-40**'s first half and **NEW-20** with one implementation. `recordFailedAttempt()` returns its post-increment count so the decision uses a fresh value. **(c)** `admin/login.php` gains an IP ceiling that **reuses** the shipped `RateLimiter::recordAuthFailure()` / `isAuthBlocked()`. **(d)** A lock that cannot be acquired within its deadline **refuses the login attempt**; an unreadable or undecodable counter file is treated as empty and logged loudly, never as "deny everyone". **(e)** **NEW-44** is fixed in path.
- **Why the audit's own recorded fix shape is refused, and this is the load-bearing part.** NEW-40 says *"the codebase already has the primitive — `ActionScheduler::acquireLock()` is a flock-based critical section. Wrap read-through-write in it."* Both halves fail against source:
  - `acquireLock()` (`action-scheduler.php:786`) is **private** and takes `LOCK_EX | LOCK_NB` — *skip if busy*, returning `null`. That is right for a scheduler and wrong for a counter: under the parallel brute force this fix exists to stop, every concurrent attempt would fail to acquire and skip its increment. That is a **deterministic** lost update, strictly worse than the racy one being replaced.
  - The read and the write are **not adjacent**. `isLockedOut()` runs at `auth.php:101` and `recordFailedAttempt()` at `:175`, with `UserManager::authenticate()` between them — which since the NEW-39 equalization performs a bcrypt verify on **every** branch, measured in D-056's own review at ~218 ms. A lock spanning read-to-write would serialise every login attempt on the install behind a 218 ms critical section: a denial-of-service lever built by a hardening slice.
- **So the criterion decides it, not the remedy (L-014).** The requirement is *a parallelized brute force must not get more than the nominal attempts*. That is met by making each counter atomic **within itself** — so every attempt is counted exactly once — and by closing the ~218 ms window with the IP ceiling rather than with a wider lock. The bcrypt figure is re-derived in the slice rather than quoted (L-015).
- **`ActionScheduler` keeps its own lock deliberately, and both sites say so.** Absorbing it would change a third subsystem's semantics from skip-if-busy to wait-then-act inside a hardening sprint — the tangling D-025, D-026, D-029 and D-031 each refused in turn. Two locks with genuinely different contracts is not duplication; two locks with the same contract would be, and that is what the primitive prevents.
- **Reusing `MCP\RateLimiter` for the IP ceiling is not a contradiction of D-056, and the distinction is recorded so it does not read as one.** D-056's implementation note 3 assessed this class for the **per-account** lockout and rejected it, because expressing 5-attempts/15-minutes through it would have meant changing `WINDOW_SECONDS` and `MAX_AUTH_FAILURES` for the MCP surface too — weakening a shipped control to avoid writing thirty lines. That reason does not apply here: the ceiling wants exactly the semantics already shipped (10 auth failures per 60 s, IP-keyed), `server.php:87,101` already consumes them, and **no constant moves**. This is the reuse rule applied, not bypassed.
- **Why the fail directions differ, stated rather than left to look inconsistent.** A lock that cannot be taken and a file that cannot be parsed are different failures. Not counting an attempt is precisely the amplification being closed, so a lock timeout **refuses** — the legitimate user retries and the lock is held for microseconds. Refusing everyone because a counter file is corrupt would instead make one unreadable file a total login outage, i.e. hand an attacker with write access to `data/` a denial of service in exchange for a race they could already only partly win. So a corrupt file starts a fresh counter and says so in the log.
- **NEW-44 is fixed in path because this slice would otherwise reproduce it.** `admin-gate.php:282,296` pass `'security'` as `$source`, and `Logger::write():122` drops any source that is not `'core'` unless a **plugin** with that ID has logging enabled — so every default-deny refusal of the S-07 gate writes nothing, Developer Mode on or off. This slice adds refusal logging of its own; adding it with the same mistake would be the fourth appearance of one defect. The minimal fix is taken — the callers pass `'core'` and keep the category in the message and context — rather than teaching `Logger` a reserved-source list, which is a contract change belonging to the **NEW-32** logging slice.
- **The concurrency test is the deliverable, not a formality.** NEW-20 has been carried since Sprint 1 slice 7 explicitly as *plausible and unproven*, because "neither the reviewer nor this slice ran the concurrency test that would settle it". This slice runs it: N parallel processes against one bucket, observed producing lost updates against the unfixed tree **before** its silence on the fixed tree is allowed to mean anything (L-016).
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **the audit's `acquireLock()` shape** (refuted above on two independent grounds); **one lock spanning `Auth::login()`** (genuinely removes the window, and serialises every login behind ~218 ms of bcrypt — a DoS lever); **fail open on a lock timeout** (today's behaviour, and exactly the hole: anyone able to provoke contention gets uncounted attempts); **fail closed on an unreadable file** (symmetrical-looking and wrong — it converts file corruption into a total login outage); **a new IP limiter of our own** (a second implementation of a control this product already ships, which this project treats as a defect); **fixing NEW-44 by teaching `Logger` reserved core sources** (better end state, and it is a contract change that belongs to the NEW-32 slice).
- Supersedes: the recorded fix shape inside audit **NEW-40** only. Closes **NEW-40**, **NEW-20** and **NEW-44**; opens **NEW-45**.
- **Implementation note 1 (slice 1, completing the slice) — the ceiling refused with the WRONG WORDS, and the owed test is what found it.** The first implementation of item (c) set `$error = __( 'auth.too_many_attempts' )` inside the throttle branch of `admin/login.php`. The refusal-wording block below it then ran unconditionally, took its `else` — `'throttled'` does not start with `account_locked:` — and **overwrote the message with `auth.login_failed`**. So a throttled caller was answered **HTTP 429 carrying the words "Incorrect username or password"**, and the `auth.too_many_attempts` key this same slice added to all **20** catalogues was unreachable in the product. Fixed by giving the page exactly one place that turns a refusal into words: the branch sets only `$result['error']`, and the block below maps it. Recorded rather than folded in silently for two reasons. First, `scripts/keel-verify`'s locale check could not have caught it — catalogue **parity** was perfect and the string was simply never rendered, which is **L-019's family**: the mechanism existed and the outcome did not. Second, it is the direct vindication of the slice being held open: the code, the i18n and the docs were all green and committed, and the defect lived in precisely the gap the three owed tests were owed for.
- **Implementation note 2 (slice 1) — the fail-closed branch and the gate's logging are proven from OUTSIDE the process that implements them.** `Auth::login()`'s `account_locked:` answer on an unrecordable failure is driven by a real second process holding `LOCK_EX` on `data/login_lockouts.json` (the `hold` mode added to `tests/fixtures/file-lock-worker.php`), not by a stubbed primitive — a mock would assert the mock. NEW-44 is proven by **reading the log file**, never the call, because reading the call is what said it worked for the whole life of the defect; and its twin refusal, the unresolvable-path branch that no HTTP request can reach, is pinned by a source scan in `AdminGateMapTest` asserting neither `klytos_log_*()` call in `admin-gate.php` passes a third argument at all. That scan is deliberately scoped to that one file: passing a plugin ID as `$source` is the correct, documented use of the parameter elsewhere in core, so a repository-wide version would be wrong rather than merely broad.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-26, before commit — L-015): both subagents ran on the finished diff, docs included. NEITHER returned a blocking finding, and between them they produced one measured correction to this decision's own claim, one FALSE claim in the approved sprint plan, and three new audit findings.** Recorded rather than folded in silently, because two of the items are corrections to my own text.
  - **The `security-auditor`'s main finding was a hypothesis, and MEASURING it confirmed it — so this decision's claim is narrowed rather than defended.** D-059 says the ~218 ms window is *"closed"* by the IP ceiling. It is not closed; it is bounded. The ceiling has the same check-then-act shape as the counters it replaces — `isAuthBlocked()` reads, `Auth::login()` spends ~218 ms on bcrypt, and only then does `recordAuthFailure()` write — so concurrent requests all observe "under the limit" before any has recorded. Measured with the bucket pre-filled to 9 of 10, one process per request, running the real sequence: **6 sequential → 1 served (bucket 10); 6 simultaneous → 6 served (bucket 15); 12 simultaneous → 12 served (bucket 21)**. The sequential run is the control that proves the instrument can see the difference. **No increment is lost** — the bucket lands on exactly 9 + N, which is what this decision's atomicity actually bought — so this is a check-then-act window, not NEW-20's lost update. Recorded as **NEW-46** with its fix shape (count the attempt, not the failure — a policy change needing its own decision) and stated with the numbers in `docs/reference/authentication.md`. **The auditor flagged it explicitly as unproven; this project settles those by execution (L-016), and the measurement is what turned a plausible note into a bounded, quantified limit rather than either a dismissal or an alarm.**
  - **My own measurement of that measurement was wrong first, and the disagreement is what caught it (L-023).** The probe's served-count used `grep -c` over result files carrying no trailing newline, so every worker's answer concatenated onto one line and the count came back **1** while the bucket said **15**. Two numbers about one event disagreed; the counter was the broken half. Before that, the probe's `require` path was wrong and it fataled loudly — the same broken-require that produced last session's false 0/20, caught this time because it screamed instead of returning a plausible zero.
  - **The `code-reviewer` found a claim in the APPROVED sprint plan that was simply false, and it was the mitigation for a recorded risk.** `docs/sprints/sprint-6.md` risk 1 said *"The ceiling is filterable"*, offered as the operator's remedy for **NEW-17** (a whole office behind one NAT address sharing the bucket). Re-derived: `installer/admin/login.php` and `installer/core/mcp/rate-limiter.php` contained **zero** `klytos_apply_filters` calls. **The filter was added rather than the sentence softened** — a mitigation an approved plan promises is part of the plan, not a description of it. `auth.login_ip_blocked` filters the decision at the login call site **only**: filtering inside `MCP\RateLimiter` would move constants shared with `core/mcp/server.php`, which is exactly what D-056's note 3 and this decision both refuse. It cannot reach the per-account lockout. **Proven with a REAL subscriber** — a plugin written into `installer/plugins/`, activated by writing plugin state the way the seeder does, and loaded by the product's own `PluginLoader` inside the SERVER process — because a filter registered in the test process would prove nothing about the process that serves the request, and **L-019** exists in this project because a hook with no reachable subscriber once read as a working feature. Proven in both directions: with the plugin active the burst is served, and with it gone the same burst is refused.
  - **Two further findings recorded, not fixed, each verified against source first.** **NEW-47** — the password-login POST runs no CSRF check, so an attacker holding their own account on the install can log a victim's browser into it; `SameSite=Strict` does not prevent it, because the victim has no session cookie to withhold. Pre-existing, and recorded **because this slice's own test now pins the absence** — a test asserting a property nobody decided is a decision made by omission. **NEW-48** — the 2FA emergency-email branch sets `security.no_email` and the shared outcome block below then overwrites it with `security.2fa_invalid_code`, so a user with no email on file is told their code is invalid. That is implementation note 1's defect one branch away in the same file; not fixed, because it is the 2FA handler and not the password-login handler this slice changed (D-031's narrowing, as applied to NEW-45 at this sprint's kickoff).
  - **NEW-17 was extended rather than duplicated.** The auditor found the entry only ever covered the *availability* half. In the case `getClientIp()` does trust — a loopback proxy — it takes the first, client-supplied `X-Forwarded-For` entry with no trusted-hop allow-list, so a proxy that appends (nginx's default) leaves the bucket key attacker-controlled: a per-request fresh bucket, or a deliberately filled bucket for a chosen victim. Same one remedy, so it is a second half of NEW-17 rather than a new finding, with its severity raised.
  - **A stale document that contained its own disproof was fixed.** `docs/reference/authentication.md`'s "Known limits" still said the lockout's read-modify-write is not atomic and that nothing throttles the endpoint — both false since this slice — while the same file's body, 100 lines above, described both fixes correctly. Worse, it recommended `ActionScheduler::acquireLock()` as the fix shape, **the exact remedy this decision refuted on two independent grounds**. A reader consulting the section named for exactly that purpose would have concluded the opposite of what the product does and acted on a remedy recorded as harmful.
  - **Two non-blocking items taken:** the lock-holding worker's sentinel cleanup moved into a `finally`, so a fixture cannot litter the install's real data directory when its driver dies first; and the source-scan matcher in `AdminGateMapTest` gained a docblock stating its known limit (it counts literal brackets with no string context, and works today only because both log messages' embedded `(security)` is balanced) rather than leaving the fragility implicit.
  - **One style note deliberately NOT taken, with the reason recorded.** The reviewer flagged that the new `if ($result['error'] === 'throttled') {` lacks the project's spaces-inside-parentheses convention. It joins an `if`/`elseif` chain whose other arms are pre-existing and unspaced; spacing only the new arm would leave one statement mixed. **D-053's review amendment settled the general question in the opposite direction** — the recorded convention outranks a neighbouring file's habit — so this is recorded as a judgement about one chain rather than as a precedent: the `__( 'auth.too_many_attempts' )` call inside it IS spaced, matching its neighbours.
  - **Confirmed sound by both reviewers against source, not merely read:** every path through the password-login POST sets `$result` before it is read and no path renders the wrong message or none; the 429 introduces no account-existence oracle (the lockout is keyed by the submitted username, bcrypt cost is equalized on every branch, and the ceiling is keyed by IP, so the 429/200 split was already visible before this fix and reveals nothing about whether an account exists); the throttled branch reaches nothing privileged and skips the bcrypt entirely; the gate's new log entry discloses nothing beyond its readers' existing access (`admin/logs.php` is `site.configure`, owner and admin, and the `script` value is never request-controlled); the playground snapshot covers `rate_limits.json`, `login_lockouts.json` and `data/config/site.json.enc`, so none of the new tests leak state; and the source-parity test genuinely matches the shipped form field for field.

## D-060 — A suspended user's OAuth token is refused at authentication, not at the gate
- Date / phase: 2026-07-26 / Phase 5, Sprint 6 kickoff (user decision, taken before anything was written)
- Decision: `TokenAuth::resolveUserActor()` (`installer/core/mcp/token-auth.php`) reads the record's `status` alongside its `role` and yields **null** for any non-active account, and the OAuth branch of `TokenAuth::validate()` requires a non-null actor to accept. *(Line citations removed after the slice's `security-auditor` pass: this entry was written at the kickoff citing `:227` and `:132-139`, correct at that moment and stale the instant the fix inserted lines into the same file. Named by method instead, so the next insertion cannot rot them — the D-053 treatment.)* A suspended user's OAuth access token therefore answers **401 Unauthorized** on the next request rather than continuing to work for up to an hour. Active revocation of the stored token is **not** built.
- **Why 401 and not 403, which was the smaller diff.** D-056's implementation note 1 moved the application-password credential exactly one layer earlier for exactly this reason: *"a credential the store cannot attribute denies, and it denies at authentication (HTTP 401) instead of at the authorization gate (HTTP 403)"*. Leaving OAuth to fail at the gate would give the three credential types three different answers to one operator action, and the audit entry for NEW-41 says plainly that the inconsistency is more dangerous than the gap — an operator who suspends an account will reasonably believe access is gone. One resolver, one answer.
- **One change covers both credential types because D-047 already built the resolver that way.** `resolveUserActor()` is the single DRY point for application passwords (`validate()`'s Basic-Auth branch) and OAuth (its bearer branch). For app passwords this is defence in depth — `validateAppPassword()` has required an active record since D-056 — and for OAuth it is the fix.
- **The consequence nobody asked for, stated rather than discovered:** an OAuth token whose user record is **gone** also moves from 403 to 401. That is the same direction D-056 chose for the same condition on the app-password path, and both still fail closed; only the layer moves.
- **Why revocation is refused here.** Deleting a suspended user's tokens is what an operator ultimately wants, and it couples `UserManager::update()` to the OAuth token store and raises a second question the audit already named — whether existing tokens should also be invalidated on a **role** change. That is its own decision with its own test point. `docs/reference/mcp-authorization.md` will state which of the two this slice delivers rather than implying the stronger one, because claiming revocation while shipping deny-at-validation would be the L-002 defect.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **null actor only, 403 at the gate** (smallest diff and zero risk to the authentication path — but it leaves three credentials with three behaviours under one suspension, which is the finding); **401 plus revocation on suspension** (what an operator expects, and it is a lifecycle change with its own decision and test point — it would widen a sprint already carrying three findings); **shortening `ACCESS_LIFETIME`** (reduces the window without closing it, and slows every legitimate client to buy a fraction of the property a status check gives outright).
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **NEW-41**; extends **D-047**'s resolver and mirrors **D-056**'s layer choice.
- **Implementation note 1 (slice 2) — an existing test pinned the OLD layer in its precondition, and it is a spec correction, not a test fix.** `McpActorResolutionTest::testOAuthTokenForAnUnknownSubjectResolvesToNoActor` asserted `validate() === true` *and* `getActor() === null` — i.e. "the OAuth token itself authenticates; the store simply cannot attribute it". That is precisely the layer this decision moves, so the assertion went red the moment the change landed. **This is D-056's implementation note 1 arriving one layer up, on the same class of test**: the PROPERTY the test exists for — an OAuth token whose subject is not a usable user must deny rather than escalate — is unchanged and is now satisfied **more strictly**, at authentication (401) instead of at the gate (403). Recorded here first, then changed, per Phase 5's "a wrong test is a spec correction". The test is **corrected, never weakened**: it now asserts `validate()` is **false** AND `getActor()` is null, so it still fails if such a token were ever to authenticate, and it fails in a second way if one were ever to carry an actor. Its class docblock's sentence about "a credential that still authenticates but cannot be attributed" was narrowed in the same edit to name the case where that remains true — a bearer token with no stamped role — because after this slice it is no longer true of OAuth.
- **Implementation note 2 (slice 2) — the fall-through, and why it is not an early `return false`.** The OAuth branch now sets `authMethod`/`authIdentifier`/`actor` only once a non-null actor exists, and otherwise falls through to step 3 (Basic Auth) rather than returning false immediately. That preserves the method's documented "tries in order" contract: a request carrying both a rejected OAuth token and valid Basic credentials for an active account still authenticates as that account. A bearer-only request — every real one — reaches step 3, finds nothing, and returns false, which `server.php` answers as **401**. Stated because "fall through" and "return false" are indistinguishable on the wire for the case under test and distinguishable for the case that is not.
- **Implementation note 3 (slice 2) — the status expression is the login path's, not a second one.** The guard is `( $user['status'] ?? 'active' ) !== 'active'`, byte-for-byte the expression `UserManager::authenticate()` uses (`user-manager.php:451`). A record with no `status` key therefore reads as active on both paths. Writing a stricter spelling here (deny when the key is absent) would have created a second definition of "is this account usable" — free to drift from the one the login form asks — which is the S-04 defect this project has already paid for once.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-26, before commit — L-015): both subagents ran in parallel on the finished diff, docs included, and NEITHER returned a blocking finding.** Recorded rather than folded in silently, because one of the three items taken is a real behavioural consequence of this slice that no one had written down, and another is a claim of mine that was argued rather than proven.
  - **The `security-auditor` found a consequence of the fix that is not a security boundary and IS an operability property, and it is now recorded as NEW-49.** A refused OAuth request is an *authentication failure*, so `server.php:101` now feeds `RateLimiter`'s auth-failure bucket where a suspended user's token previously answered 200 and fed nothing. **Re-verified against source before recording** (L-013/L-015): that bucket is keyed `'ip:' . $ip` (`rate-limiter.php:105-138`), shared by every credential from the address, and `isAuthBlocked()` runs at the top of **every** MCP request (`server.php:87`) — so a suspended integration with a retry loop can push a shared NAT or reverse-proxy address past 10 failures per 60 s and answer **429** to every other MCP client behind it. **It is not a new attacker capability, and that was checked rather than assumed:** any garbage bearer token already reaches the identical call at the identical cost of one unauthenticated request. What is new is that a legitimate, previously silent client can now cause it by accident. Severity LOW, bounded by the same shared-address precondition as **NEW-17**, whose remedy it waits on. Stated in `docs/reference/mcp-authorization.md` beside the behaviour that causes it, so an operator meets it where they would look.
  - **The fall-through of implementation note 2 was a documented claim with no test, and it now has one.** The auditor could find nothing pinning "a rejected OAuth token does not suppress an independently valid Basic credential", which is exactly the shape L-014 warns about — a behaviour asserted in a comment and in this decision, believed because it was written down. `McpActorResolutionTest::testARejectedOAuthTokenDoesNotBlockAValidBasicCredential` now drives it with a suspended subject's OAuth token in the `Authorization` header and an active account's application password in `PHP_AUTH_USER`/`PHP_AUTH_PW` (the two cannot share one header), asserting the method is `app_password`, the role is the Basic credential's, and nothing from the rejected branch leaks. **Proven in both directions:** replacing the fall-through with an early `return false` makes exactly this test fail ("Failed asserting that false is true") and nothing else, and the file was restored from a byte-for-byte backup afterwards.
  - **Line citations in this very entry had rotted, and the fix is the D-053 one.** D-060's Decision line cited `token-auth.php:227` and `validate():132-139`; both were correct when written at the kickoff and stale the instant this slice inserted lines into the same file (`resolveUserActor()` is now at `:252`). Corrected to name the method rather than the line, here and in `docs/sprints/sprint-6.md`, so the next insertion cannot rot them again.
  - **The `code-reviewer` returned no blocking finding and confirmed by reading what the slice claims:** `authMethod`/`authIdentifier`/`actor` are reset at the top of every `validate()` and assigned only inside the branch that succeeds, so no path leaves half-set identity; `Helpers::jsonResponse()` is `: never`, so `server.php`'s 401 terminates the request and cannot fall through to `setActor()` with a null actor; the status expression is byte-identical to the **three** other "is this account usable" checks in the codebase (`user-manager.php:451`, `auth.php:341`, `auth.php:762`), so no second semantic was introduced; `resolveUserActor()` has exactly two call sites and `getActor()` one; and the spec correction is strictly stronger than the assertion it replaced. Its one informational note is recorded rather than acted on: the new 401 path fires **no** hook, where the 403 gate has `mcp.access_denied` — a pre-existing gap in the resolver dating to D-047, outside this diff under D-031's narrowing.
  - **Both reviewers ran without a shell and said so unprompted.** The limit is recorded rather than glossed: neither could execute `phpunit`, `phpcs` or `keel-verify`, so every number in this slice rests on measurements taken in the main session. Where they could verify by reading — the branch analysis, the four status expressions, the call-site enumeration, the doc-versus-code comparison — they did, and those conclusions are theirs.
- **Implementation note 4 (slice 2) — `mcpCall()` was promoted, not copied.** The MCP HTTP request helper lived as a private method in `McpGateHttpTest` (Sprint 2 slice 2). This slice is the second MCP HTTP test class, so it moved to `tests/AdminHttpTestCase.php` as a protected method and the original now calls it — the same generalization, for the same recorded reason, as `routerScript()` in slice 6: a second copy would fork the three defects **L-008** records the moment one copy is fixed and the other is not.

## D-061 — NEW-47 and NEW-26 are pulled into Sprint 6 as slice 4, and the CSRF primitive itself is fixed in path
- Date / phase: 2026-07-27 / Phase 5, Sprint 6 (scope change — **explicit user decision**, taken on a question put at the slice-2 close and answered "adelanta la NEW-47")
- Decision: **(a)** The password-login POST in `installer/admin/login.php` verifies CSRF, closing audit **NEW-47**; the field is emitted by the shipped form through `klytos_csrf_field()`. **(b)** `installer/admin/reset-password.php`'s set-password POST does the same, closing **NEW-26** — the two are one defect class on one flow and close together. **(c)** `Auth::validateCsrf()` refuses an empty token or an empty expected value, closing **NEW-50**, found while proving (a) and fixed in path because (a) does not work without it. **(d)** The refusal is **HTTP 403 plus the re-rendered form**, worded from the ONE mapping D-059 implementation note 1 established, with a new locale key `auth.session_expired` in all 20 catalogues.
- **Why this is a scope change and not a slice detail:** Sprint 6 was planned and approved with three slices (NEW-40/NEW-20, NEW-41, NEW-42). This adds a fourth, ahead of slice 3, on the user's instruction. Recorded as a decision with its own estimate version rather than absorbed, per the scope-change playbook.
- **Why NEW-47 was worth pulling forward, stated in the terms that make it real:** login CSRF is not "an attacker logs into your account" — it is the reverse, which is why it reads as harmless. An attacker holding their own account on the install forces the victim's browser to submit the ATTACKER's credentials; the victim then works, unaware, inside an account the attacker controls, and everything they write, upload or connect is the attacker's to read. `SameSite=Strict` does not prevent it, because the victim has no session cookie to withhold — the request that creates one is the attack. And this project had a second reason: slice 1's source-parity test **pinned the absence**, so the gap had become a decision made by omission.
- **NEW-50 is the finding of this slice, and it was found by execution rather than by reading.** After `klytos_verify_csrf()` was added to the password branch, the token-less requests in `LoginCeilingHttpTest` were **still served**. The cause: `Helpers::verifyCsrf()` resolves a missing field to `''`, `Auth::validateCsrf()` compared it against a session value that is also `''` for an anonymous visitor, and **`hash_equals( '', '' )` returns TRUE** — measured, not assumed. So the check passed for exactly the state the login and password-reset forms live in, and every admin surface shared the hole: any POST arriving in a session that had never rendered a form was accepted with no token at all. The guard is placed in `validateCsrf()` — the one place that decides whether a token is valid — rather than as a `!== ''` at the login call site, because a local guard leaves every other caller with the hole. That is the S-04/S-07 shape, and this project has paid for it twice already.
- **Why 403 and re-render, not a redirect or a bare error:** the browser needs the form back to retry, and the throttle branch three lines away already answers this way (429 + re-render, D-059). One shape per page.
- **Why the wording is about the SESSION and not the credentials:** the realistic trigger for a legitimate user is a page left open until the session expired. Telling them "Incorrect username or password" would send them to change a password that was never wrong. `auth.session_expired` says what happened and what to do.
- **The 2FA branch's message was changed in the same edit, and that is in scope by necessity rather than opportunism:** it has verified CSRF since before this slice and answered the generic `common.error`. Leaving it would have meant one form on one page explaining the problem and its sibling saying "Error" — an inconsistency **created by this change**, so it is corrected with it. Nothing else in that branch is touched (D-031's narrowing; NEW-48 stays open with its trigger).
- **The test harness had to change with the product, and that was predicted rather than discovered.** `LoginCeilingHttpTest`'s source-parity test — written in slice 1 precisely for this — failed on the same run that added the field, naming the cause: *"The password form now emits a CSRF token that these requests do not send."* Its requests and `AuthLoginHttpTest`'s now fetch the session and the token **from the page itself** (`AdminHttpTestCase::formSession()`), never mint them: a harness-minted token would prove the harness agrees with itself, which is L-026 with the sign flipped. The parity assertion was inverted in the same slice and says so in place, so the flip is a record rather than a silent edit.
- **Proven in three separate TEMP-BREAK cycles**, each reverted from a byte-for-byte backup confirmed with `cmp`: the login check removed → the forged login **succeeded with 302** (three tests red); `validateCsrf()`'s guard removed → the token-less and empty-token logins were served and the reset form accepted (three red), which is what makes NEW-50 load-bearing rather than tidy; the reset check removed → the reset form accepted a token-less POST (one red).
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **NEW-47 alone, NEW-26 later** (smaller — but the two are the same defect class on the same flow and the second would need the same locale key, the same test shape and a second review round); **a `!== ''` guard in `login.php` only** (closes the login hole and leaves every other caller of `validateCsrf()` accepting an empty token — the by-omission shape this project has closed three times); **a redirect instead of a re-render** (loses the message and turns a refusal into something a caller cannot distinguish from success); **reusing `common.error`** (free, and it tells a locked-out user nothing); **a token bound to the form rather than the session** (a second CSRF mechanism beside the one every other surface uses — the duplication rule refuses it).
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **NEW-47**, **NEW-26** and **NEW-50**. Extends **D-059**'s "one place turns a refusal into words" to a third refusal on the same page.

## D-062 — The road to v1 is recorded, in this order
- Date / phase: 2026-07-27 / Phase 5 (explicit user decision — the order was proposed with reasons and approved: *"crea toda la ruta"*)
- Decision: `docs/roadmap.md` is created and is the project's ordered plan to v1: **(1)** NEW-27 (the `.gitattributes` review) — first; **(2)** finish the hardening; **(3)** accessibility; **(4)** the theme package (D-023/D-024); **(5)** Phase 6; **(6)** Phase 7; **(7)** Phase 8, on request. Each step carries what it contains, why it sits there, and what it unblocks.
- **Why an ordered document exists at all:** the inventory of open work has always been recoverable — `docs/04-adoption-audit.md` and `docs/PROGRESS.md` hold it — but the ORDER was not written down anywhere, so every session re-derived it and proposed it again. That is a real recurring cost in a project whose per-session state re-read is already its dominant expense.
- **Why NEW-27 is first even though it is the smallest item:** it is one line of `.gitattributes`, and until it lands the 16 in-product guides ship **empty** in every release archive while the MCP tools' own descriptions declare five of them REQUIRED before creating page content. It is also the recorded prerequisite for the bilingual guides the user asked for on 2026-07-25 — translating first would duplicate content nobody receives.
- **Why accessibility sits above the theme package and not below it:** the measured baseline is ~20–25 % (admin) and ~15 % (generated output) against the WCAG 2.2 AA + EAA target recorded in **D-007**, and under the EAA the exposure for the generated output lands on Klytos's users. The theme package is what makes A-05 fixable at the SYSTEM level rather than site by site, so the two are sequenced deliberately: the admin half of accessibility does not wait for it, and the generated-output half is finished by it.
- **What the roadmap deliberately does NOT do:** it does not re-open any recorded decision, it does not commit to dates, and it does not promise a v1 scope beyond what the audit and PROGRESS already record. It is an ordering, and the user changes it by saying so.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **no document, decide each sprint** (what has been happening — it costs a re-derivation every session and leaves the next chat to guess); **a full dated plan** (this project has no delivery date and inventing one would be fiction); **putting the theme package first** (it is the largest un-estimated item and would park the hardening and accessibility work behind a redesign).
- Supersedes: none. Bounds nothing; it orders what `docs/04-adoption-audit.md`, `docs/PROGRESS.md` and D-023/D-024 already contain.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-27, before commit — L-015): both subagents ran in parallel on the finished diff, docs included. The `code-reviewer` returned ONE BLOCKING finding, the `security-auditor` reached the same conclusion independently as its single non-blocking observation, and both were right.** Recorded in full, because what the finding led to is larger than the finding.
  - **BLOCKING, and correct: a THIRD anonymous login form.** `installer/core/mcp/oauth-authorize-view.php`'s `action === 'login'` branch calls `Auth::login()` with **no CSRF check**, and its form emits no token — while its sibling `action === 'authorize'` branch has verified CSRF all along. `Auth::login()` has exactly **two** call sites in the product, and this slice had closed one of them. The reviewer's sharpest point was about the documentation: `docs/reference/authentication.md`'s new sentence said *"Both forms a person can reach without logging in… verify a CSRF token"*, which this made **false as written** — the L-002 defect, in a document created by the same diff. Recorded as **NEW-51** and **fixed in path**, because closing one of two identical paths is the failure D-041's own review cycle wrote down; the doc now carries a three-row table naming the consent screen.
  - **And requesting that page to prove the fix found something far worse: NEW-52.** `/oauth/authorize` answered `Fatal error: Uncaught Error: Call to undefined function Klytos\Core\handleOAuthAuthorizeView()` — the router calls the view's function unqualified from `Klytos\Core` while it is declared in `Klytos\Core\MCP`, and PHP falls back to the global namespace, never to a sibling. **The OAuth consent screen has never rendered for anybody**, so the authorization-code flow could not be completed by any MCP client, ever. Confirmed **byte-identical at HEAD** before anything was concluded, so it is pre-existing. Fixed in path (one token) for the NEW-16 reason: the CSRF fix on that page cannot be proven while the page fatals. Recorded as **L-027** — *before adding a control to a surface, request that surface once* — and it also reframes **NEW-38**, which describes a 2FA gap on a page nobody could open.
  - **My own test marker was wrong, and the failure was the instrument rather than the product.** The refusal test asserted the response did not contain `Authorize`; that word is also the page's heading (*"Authorize Application"*), so the assertion fired on a correct response and, in a different ordering, passed on a wrong one. Settled by driving the sequence by hand — where it worked — then by dumping the real bodies, then by asserting on `value="authorize"` and `value="login"`, which exist only in the consent and login forms respectively. **L-018's shape** (a blocklist word that can legitimately appear), caught because a test that passed alone failed in its class — the same tell as slice 1's log-directory measurement.
  - **The two paren-spacing notes, one taken and one not, both on the record.** `Auth::validateCsrf()`'s new guard was written `if ($expected === '' …)` and is now `if ( $expected === '' … )`: a standalone statement, so **D-053's rule applies** — the project's recorded convention outranks a neighbouring file's habit. The new `'csrf'` arm in `login.php`'s wording map is deliberately left unspaced, because it is the head of an `if`/`elseif` chain whose other arms are pre-existing and unspaced, and spacing one arm leaves the statement mixed — the same judgement D-059's review amendment recorded for the same chain.
  - **Confirmed sound against source by the reviewers, not merely read:** no branch was lost when the CSRF guard re-indented ~60 lines of the login POST (`$result` is set on every path before the wording block reads it); the `validateCsrf()` guard has no caller that relied on the old empty/empty pass, because `Auth::login()` seeds `klytos_csrf` on both the authenticated and the 2FA-pending branches and `complete2fa()` seeds it again; `reset-password.php`'s chain still covers every case; the new `$sessionId` parameters are additive and no existing call site passes them; `session_regenerate_id( true )` still runs on both login paths, so starting the anonymous session earlier introduces no fixation; the 403 discloses nothing the previous behaviour did not; and the CSRF token never reaches a URL, a redirect or a log.
  - **Both reviewers ran without a shell and said so unprompted**, so every number in this slice was measured in the main session — and the finding that mattered was proven there too, with a `curl`.

## D-063 — Clone detection only when both counters are non-zero; the origin rule becomes one rule; the second skip-list stops matching basenames
- Date / phase: 2026-07-27 / Phase 5, Sprint 6 slice 3
- Decision: audit **NEW-42** is closed in four parts. **(a)** `TwoFactor::verifyPasskeyAssertion()` compares the presented signature counter with the stored one and refuses the assertion when the presented value does not exceed it — **but only when BOTH values are greater than zero** (see implementation note 2: this is deliberately more permissive than WebAuthn §7.2, which guards on OR) — firing a new `user.passkey_clone_detected` action with `( $userId, $credentialId, $storedCount, $presentedCount )`. **(b)** The origin rule moves into ONE private helper, `TwoFactor::originIsAcceptable()`, and both ceremonies call it; the assertion path gains the check it never had, including registration's `https://localhost[:port]` allowance, carried over verbatim. **(c)** A `strlen( $authData ) < 37` guard runs before any offset is read, matching registration's identical check. **(d)** The setup-wizard skip-list in `admin/bootstrap.php` moves off basename matching onto `klytos_admin_gate_key()`, exactly as `$preAuthScripts` did in D-058, and gains `api/webauthn-challenge.php`.
- **The `> 0` condition is the whole decision, and getting it wrong is worse than leaving the gap open.** Synced platform passkeys — iCloud Keychain, Google Password Manager, and every authenticator that exists in more than one place by design — report `signCount = 0` permanently. The obvious spelling, *"the new count must exceed the stored one"*, would therefore refuse the **second login of most authenticators in use today**: a security fix that breaks authentication, which is the trap **D-044** recorded when a CSP tidy-up would have broken 2FA. This was settled by execution rather than by argument: with the naive rule installed, **exactly one test failed and it was `testASyncedPasskeyReportingZeroForeverStillCompletesLogin`**, while both clone tests still passed. That is the shape a correct rule must have, and it is now pinned in both directions.
- **A counter regression is not proof of a clone, and the response is sized to that.** A restored authenticator backup produces the identical signal. So the product refuses that one assertion and announces it; it does **not** revoke the credential, notify the account holder, or lock the account. Anything stronger would turn a restore into a lockout, and it would be a lifecycle decision with its own test point rather than a rider here.
- **`user.passkey_clone_detected` has NO core listener, and that is stated in those words** in `docs/reference/authentication.md`, in the code beside the call, and in the audit entry — because **L-019** was recorded when "it goes to the audit log" was true of a hook and false of the system. The refusal happens with or without a subscriber; the hook is how a deployment finds out. It is pinned by a test that registers a real listener and asserts both counters arrive, driven **in-process** — over HTTP the product runs in another process, where a listener registered by the test could never be reached, which is exactly the kind of assertion that cannot fail.
- **Why the origin rule became a method instead of a second copy of four lines.** Registration validated the origin and assertion did not, and the asymmetry ran the wrong way: enrolment happens once per authenticator, assertion happens at every login. Copying the four lines would have closed the gap and left the two free to drift on the next edit — the **S-04** shape this project has already paid for once, and the same reasoning that produced `Helpers::webauthnRpId()` in D-058. Refactoring the registration call site is therefore part of the fix, not scope creep: it is what makes the symmetry structural instead of remembered.
- **It cannot lock out an existing user, and that is by construction rather than by hope.** Every stored credential was enrolled through the identical check, so an origin that passes enrolment passes assertion; one that would fail assertion could never have produced a credential. The pre-existing quirk that the allowance is `https://localhost[:port]` and not `http://` is inherited unchanged and now written down in "Known limits" rather than discovered by an operator — narrowing or widening the development allowance is a decision of its own.
- **The length guard's user-visible outcome is the WARNING, not the refusal**, which is why the test asserts the warning's absence. The path already failed closed; it did so by way of `ord( $authData[32] )` reading past the end of a 32-byte string, and that input is trivially precomputable because authenticator data begins with `sha256( rpId )` and the rpId is public. `phpunit.xml` has carried `failOnWarning="true"` since **D-054**, so the assertion is the suite's exit code. That test runs **in-process** deliberately: over HTTP the warning would be raised in the server's process and land in a log nobody reads.
- **`$currentScript` is deleted, and the exit condition was established before deleting it (L-007).** `admin/bootstrap.php`'s file-scope variables leak into every admin file that requires it, so "unused in this file" is not the same as unused. The whole tree was searched: after both skip-lists moved onto the gate key, nothing in PHP read it. Removing it is also what improved the core+admin lint baseline by one error — measured by linting the pre-slice file, not inferred.
- **One test fixture was corrected, and it is recorded because a reader would otherwise see a changed expectation.** `PasskeyLoginTest` emitted `signCount = 1` at enrolment AND `1` at every assertion — i.e. it modelled an authenticator that never increments, which is precisely the condition the counter exists to flag. Nothing noticed for two sprints because nothing compared the two. The counts are now named and distinct (`ENROLLED_COUNT = 1`, `NEXT_COUNT = 2`). **This is a fixture correction, not a loosened assertion:** the property `testAPasskeyCompletesASecondFactorLogin` asserts is unchanged, and it becomes the positive control for the incrementing case.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **"the new count must exceed the stored one"** (the spec-naive rule — refuted by measurement above); **refusing only on a strict regression, allowing equality** (an implementation using `<` rather than `<=` passes the regression test and accepts a replayed ceremony; a separate test now separates the two); **revoking the credential on a suspected clone** (what an operator eventually wants, and it converts an authenticator restore into a permanent lockout — its own decision, its own test point); **logging the clone instead of firing an action** (the NEW-44 trap: this project has shipped a log call that wrote nothing three times, and the logging design belongs to the **NEW-32** slice); **copying the origin check into the assertion path** (closes the gap, leaves two definitions free to drift — S-04); **keeping the basename skip-list and adding `'webauthn-challenge.php'`** (smallest diff, and it cannot express an `api/` path at all: six filenames exist in both `admin/` and `admin/api/`, so the entry would exempt every file of that name — the by-omission shape D-058 removed from the sibling list).
- Supersedes: none. Closes audit **NEW-42**.
- **Implementation note 1 — four documentation examples taught a parameter this project does not have, and they were found by checking my own.** Writing the `user.passkey_clone_detected` example I copied the shape of its neighbour and wrote `klytos_add_action( 'hook', fn, 10, 4 )`. **`klytos_add_action()` and `klytos_add_filter()` both take three parameters** (`hook, callback, priority`) — there is no WordPress-style accepted-args count, because `Hooks::doAction()` passes every argument to every listener. PHP ignores extra arguments to a userland function, so the examples *ran*; they simply taught an API that does not exist. Three were pre-existing (`authentication.md` for `user.passkey_enrolled`, `ai-runtime.md`, `public-comments.md`) and one was mine. **All four are corrected**, not only mine: leaving the sibling example three paragraphs above my own saying something different would be a document contradicting itself, which is the defect **L-024** records, and shipping two mental models of one API is **L-004**. Verified afterwards that no example anywhere in `docs/` or `.claude/skills/` still passes a fourth argument.
- **Implementation note 2 — the rule that ships is NOT what the WebAuthn specification says, and the first draft of this entry cited the specification as its authority. That citation was wrong, and I wrote it.** Verified against the published text rather than recalled: §7.2 *Verifying an Authentication Assertion* guards the counter comparison with **OR** — *"If `authData.signCount` is nonzero **or** `storedSignCount` is nonzero, then run the following sub-step"* — while this slice ships **AND**. The two agree in every case but one: **stored non-zero, presented zero**, which the spec calls a cloning signal and which Klytos accepts. Stated without softening: **an attacker holding a cloned credential can bypass this check by presenting a signature counter of zero.**
  - **Why AND still ships.** It is what `docs/sprints/sprint-6.md` recorded and the user approved, and this project does not reinterpret an approved scope mid-slice. The spec's rule is also not free: the case it catches is indistinguishable from an authenticator that legitimately *stops* incrementing — a firmware reset, or a credential migrated from a hardware key into a synced store — and refusing those users from then on is **D-044**'s trap in a narrower population. The spec itself leaves the RESPONSE to the Relying Party (*"Whether the Relying Party updates `storedSignCount` in this case, or not, or fails the authentication ceremony or not, is Relying Party-specific"*) — but it does **not** leave the CONDITION to the Relying Party, so this is a divergence and is labelled as one everywhere it appears.
  - **SETTLED 2026-07-27 — explicit user decision, after the trade-off was put to them: AND STAYS.** The question asked was whether to move to the standard's OR and refuse "stored non-zero, presented zero". **What decided it is that clone detection does not stop a determined attacker under EITHER rule:** whoever holds a cloned credential also controls the counter it presents, so knowing the rule they simply present stored + 1 and pass. OR therefore buys detection only against a *careless* clone that honestly reports its own counter — while its cost falls on real users, whose authenticator can legitimately reset (a firmware update, or a credential migrated from a hardware key into a synced store) and would then be refused permanently. The standard itself frames the counter as input to risk scoring rather than as a gate — *"Relying Parties should incorporate this information into their risk scoring"* — and leaves the response to the RP. So Klytos keeps the permissive condition, keeps refusing the classic regression, and keeps announcing every detection through the hook. **The divergence is not an oversight and is labelled as a deliberate choice wherever it appears**, with the full case table in `docs/reference/authentication.md`. A third option was offered and not taken: detect the presented-zero case but only announce it, never refuse — rejected as one rule with two different answers, for a case that catches only careless clones anyway.
  - **The general shape, because it is the third time this project has been bitten by it:** a specification citation is a factual claim like any other, and I wrote one from memory into code, an audit entry and a decision before checking it. It was caught by asking what the rule does at the edges — stored non-zero, presented zero — and then reading the actual text. **L-015**'s rule (re-derive, do not quote) applies to standards, not only to this project's own records.
- **Review amendment (2026-07-27, before commit — L-015): both subagents ran in parallel on the finished diff, docs included. NEITHER returned a blocking finding — the twenty-first consecutive slice where the round still paid.** Between them they produced one correction to my own diff, two new audit findings, and one flagged item that turned out NOT to be a defect once checked.
  - **The `code-reviewer`'s one substantive item was taken, and it was an inconsistency inside my own diff.** The new length guard was written `if (strlen($authData) < 37) {` — unspaced, because I was mirroring registration's identical line — while the origin check **six lines above it in the same diff** was written spaced. A new standalone statement takes the project's recorded style (**D-053**'s amendment, reapplied in D-059 and D-061; the unspaced exception is only for arms of a pre-existing `if`/`elseif` chain, which this is not). Now `if ( strlen( $authData ) < 37 ) {`. Worth recording because `phpcs` does **not** enforce this — the reviewer noted that too, and it is exactly why the convention needs a reader rather than a tool.
  - **NEW-53 (from the `security-auditor`): the localhost origin allowance is a prefix match**, so `https://localhost:8443@evil.example` — userinfo, host `evil.example` — and `https://localhost:8443/path` satisfy it. Recorded, not fixed: it is **pre-existing** and tightening it changes the registration path, outside this slice's four items (D-031). The boundary is **pinned by a test that asserts the loose behaviour on purpose**, the D-044 `style-src` precedent, so a later tightening reads as a deliberate inverted assertion instead of a mystery failure. I had reached the same conclusion independently while proving the refactor, which is the useful part: two routes to one finding.
  - **NEW-54 (from the `security-auditor`): a FAILED assertion does not consume its challenge** — `deleteWebAuthnChallenge()` is on the success path only, so all 12 `return false` branches leave it live for up to 300 s. Verified against source before recording (12 branches, one deletion). Pre-existing — but **this slice created the first refusal that happens AFTER a valid signature**, which is the only case where the window is reachable at all, so the entry is written now rather than left implicit.
  - **One flagged item was checked and is NOT a security defect, and saying so is the point (L-013).** The auditor noticed `$opensslAlgo = $algorithm === -257 ? OPENSSL_ALGO_SHA256 : OPENSSL_ALGO_SHA256;` — both ternary branches identical, so the declared COSE algorithm is ignored. It reads like an algorithm-confusion bug. It is not: `coseKeyToPem()` returns a PEM **only** for `(kty 2, alg -7)` ES256 and `(kty 3, alg -257)` RS256 and `null` for everything else, and the caller refuses on `null` before this line — and SHA-256 is the correct digest for **both** of those algorithms. So the ternary is dead code that happens to be right, not a hole. Recorded as redundant-and-harmless with the reason, rather than either "fixed" in a slice that does not own it or repeated as a finding it is not.
  - **The `code-reviewer`'s unguarded-`doAction` note was NOT taken, with the reason.** `klytos_do_action( 'user.passkey_clone_detected', … )` has no `try`/`catch`, and `Hooks::doAction()` has no per-listener guard, so a throwing plugin listener would escape `verifyPasskeyAssertion()`. That is the shape D-058's review called BLOCKING for `user.passkey_enrolled` — but there the failure **misreported a persisted success**, whereas here the security property (the login is not completed) holds either way, there is deliberately no core listener, and it is the same unguarded dispatch as the other 363 `doAction()` call sites. Wrapping this one call would be a local answer to a project-wide contract question, which is the by-omission shape this project keeps closing centrally instead.
  - **Both reviewers ran without a shell and said so unprompted**, so every number in this slice was measured in the main session. The `code-reviewer` explicitly flagged the one axis it could not close: whether `originIsAcceptable()` is byte-for-byte equivalent to the pre-refactor inline expression, since it could not run `git diff`. **That gap was closed by execution rather than left open** — the old expression and the new helper were run against 63 origin/rpId combinations and produced **zero** behaviour differences, which is also what makes NEW-53 provably pre-existing rather than something this slice introduced.
  - **Confirmed sound against source by the reviewers, not merely read:** the clone check sits after `openssl_verify()`, so a forged assertion can neither spam the hook nor advance the stored counter; `<=` (not `<`) correctly refuses a repeated counter as well as a regression; a missing or non-integer `sign_count` degrades to 0 rather than crashing or wrongly refusing; **37 is exactly the minimum for the three offsets the method reads** (0–31, 32, 33–36) with no off-by-one; the new wizard condition is fail-closed in the same direction as the code it replaces and `$currentSurface` is assigned on every path that reaches it; `$currentScript` has zero remaining PHP references repo-wide; the new tests differentiate real boundaries and the refusal tests additionally prove the session never reaches the dashboard; the ordered list of checks in `docs/reference/authentication.md` matches the code's actual order; the INDEX row matches the real call signature; and the slice introduces no new user-facing string, so the 20-catalogue rule does not apply.
  - **One false alarm the auditor raised and resolved itself, recorded so the next reader does not re-raise it:** several documents still say `verifyPasskeyAssertion()` "has zero call sites". They describe the state at the moment NEW-09 was found and are followed by their own CLOSED resolution text — the project's "original entry follows, unchanged" convention, not an L-002 defect.

## D-064 — The state files get an index and a reading rule; PROGRESS.md's narrative moves to history.md
- Date / phase: 2026-07-27 / Phase 5, Sprint 6 close (**explicit user decision**, taken on a question put with measured numbers and three options)
- Decision: **(a)** `docs/decisions.md` and `docs/lessons-learned.md` each gain a one-line-per-entry **Index** at the top, immediately under a short paragraph stating the reading rule: read the index in full, then open the BODY of only the entries the current work touches, the entries added since the last session, and anything the index line leaves uncertain. **(b)** `docs/PROGRESS.md`'s narrative — the per-slice record under "Current position" and the whole "Last updated"/"Previously" chain — moves verbatim to a new **`docs/history.md`**, which is explicitly NOT part of the session-start read. PROGRESS.md keeps the project card, the phase status, a short current position, the open and deferred items, and the next action. **(c) Nothing is archived, nothing is trimmed, and no entry body is edited.**
- **The numbers, because this decision is about a cost and it was measured rather than felt.** The mandatory session-start read was `docs/PROGRESS.md` + `docs/decisions.md` + `docs/lessons-learned.md` = **517 KB ≈ 129,300 tokens**, of which `decisions.md` alone was **314 KB ≈ 78,600 tokens across 63 entries**. That file went from **35 KB to 314 KB in eight days** — ninefold. After this change the same read is **≈ 28,100 tokens** (PROGRESS.md 122 KB → **21 KB**; the decisions index is **8 KB**), a saving of roughly **101,000 tokens every session, for the life of the project**.
- **Why an index and not an archive, which is the option that was rejected.** Splitting closed decisions into a `decisions-archive.md` saves more, and it requires judging at every close which entries no longer govern — a judgement that is wrong more often than it looks. **D-023, D-031 and D-036 are among the oldest entries here and all three still bind daily work.** "Closed" and "no longer governing" are different properties, and a session that skips a decision because someone mis-sorted it is exactly the re-litigation this file exists to prevent. The index keeps every entry equally reachable and costs one line each.
- **Why the reading rule lives in the file rather than in the lock.** `CLAUDE.md`'s Keel block is Keel's canonical text and is refreshed only from the skill, so it is not the place for a project convention. Putting the rule in the header of the file being read means the instruction arrives with the thing it governs and cannot drift away from it.
- **The failure mode is named rather than left to be discovered:** a session reads an index line, decides an entry is irrelevant, and proceeds against a decision that binds it. The header therefore says, in those words, that when in doubt the entry gets opened — re-opening a settled decision costs far more than one extra read. The index lines carry the full decision title, not a shortened label, precisely so the judgement is made on the real subject.
- **Verified, not assumed.** After the rewrite, the region of each file from its first entry onward was hashed and compared with a pre-change backup: **byte-identical** in both files (SHA-256 match), so the index and header are strictly additive. Entry counts re-checked at 63 and 27. `PROGRESS.md` + `history.md` sum to more than the original only by the new header text.
- Alternatives rejected (and why): **an era split into `decisions-archive.md`** (saves more; requires a per-close judgement about what still governs, which the three examples above show is not mechanical, and the cost of getting it wrong is the exact failure the file prevents); **splitting only `PROGRESS.md`** (safest and cheapest, saves ~25 k instead of ~101 k, and leaves the largest file untouched — which is what the previous four closes did, and is why this was still owed); **subject-based files** (`decisions-security.md` etc. — most decisions here span subjects, and a decision filed under the wrong subject is invisible).
- Supersedes: none. Discharges the item the Sprint 5 close deferred and `docs/roadmap.md` records as owed at the Sprint 6 close.

## D-065 — A design handoff arrived for the admin: Phases 3–4 become live and D-009 is superseded
- Date / phase: 2026-07-27 / Phase 5 → Phases 3–4 (**explicit user decision**, taken on a question put with three options; the user chose to run it properly through Keel rather than prototype one file)
- Decision: the Claude Design project **"Klytos CMS Redesign"** (`6916aa0a-…`, bundle `design_handoff_klytos_admin/` — 41 screens, a token set, a component inventory and the SVG logo family) is accepted as a real design handoff. Consequently: **(a)** **D-009 is superseded** — this project now HAS a design contract, and the phase-status table's `n/a` for Phases 3 and 4 no longer holds; **(b)** the redesign runs as its own sprint through Keel Phases 3–4 and then Phase 5, not as an edit inside the hardening sprint; **(c)** the Phase 4 Step 1 completeness gate ran immediately and its evidence is in `docs/BUILD-SPEC.md`; **(d)** the gate FAILED and **DR-001** is registered and sent, so **no admin file has been touched**; **(e)** the deferred `design-fidelity-auditor` subagent's trigger — recorded as *"the first redesign that goes through Phases 3–4"* — has **fired**.
- **Why this is a decision and not just work.** `docs/roadmap.md` (**D-062**) records the ordered route to v1 and a 41-screen admin redesign is not in it at any position — it is plausibly larger than every step in that roadmap combined. The user is entitled to reorder their own project, and did; what would have been wrong is absorbing something this size silently, which is exactly the scope-change playbook's reason for existing.
- **The gate result, in one line, because it is the load-bearing part:** the handoff is **strong on intent and incomplete against the contract**. It correctly forbids React and bundlers and states that the prototypes are references rather than code — the single most common way a handoff goes wrong, closed in its first paragraph — and its colour tokens were verified value-by-value against its own spec tables and **match exactly**.
